\section{Theoretical Model}
As reasoned before, the theoretical model in this section builds on \textcite{Isore2017} and is extended by a banking sector combining \textcite{Gertler2011} with home-bias sovereign bond holdings and the \textcite{Bernanke1999} external finance premium. It therefore consists of a representative household with capital-goods producers, a continuum of intermediate-goods firms with Calvo Pricing, a representative final-goods firm, a continuum of entrepreneurs à la \textcite{Bernanke1999} that hold the economies capital stock in a banking block with a continuum of banks à lq \textcite{Gertler2011} and a public authority. The following subsection starts by defining the model's disaster state. 

\subsection{The Disaster State}\label{sec:disaster_state}

% Uncertainty about the future is central to asset pricing, since all agents are typically risk-averse. A \textbf{\emph{state of nature}} denotes a possible realization of the world at a given point in time. In principle, the set of states of nature is continuous and thus uncountably infinite. 

% Accordingly, we first present a discrete version of the analysis, assuming discrete time $t \in \{0,1,2,\dots\}$ and a finite state space $S=\{s_1,\dots,s_n\}$. To make the analysis tractable, however, we will discretize this set, thereby simplifying reality while still providing a workable framework, in which our economy can enter two states of nature ($n = 2$), namely a disaster and "business-as-usual" (BAU) state. In the latter, the economy operates in conditions where no capital, bonds nor consumption or output are destroyed. However, there exists a small time-varying probability to enter a disaster state in which it experiences e.g. a doom loop where one economic condition creates another, resulting in another economic negative condition which then reinforces the first or second, resulting in a negatively spiralling chain reaction.

Each period, the economy faces a small but time-varying probability of a
sovereign partial default. Following \textcite{Isore2017} and
\textcite{Gourio2012}, a disaster destroys both physical capital and
productivity by the factor $(1-\Delta^k)$ when $x_{t+1}=1$.  The paper's novel mechanism adds a sovereign haircut $\Delta^b>0$ on top of this: in the disaster state bondholders are repaid only $(1-\Delta^b)$ of face value, and because sovereign bonds sit on bank balance sheets at the home-bias share $\phi$, the loss erodes bank net worth, tightens the leverage constraint of Appendix~\ref{sec:banksov}, and raises the cost of credit to entrepreneurs.  With one-period bonds this transmission requires a \emph{realised} default. A rise in $\theta_t$ alone lowers $Q^b_t$ and widens the sovereign spread on impact, but leaves bank net worth unaffected at $t$, because net worth accumulation applies the realised return $R^b_t$ to the predetermined position $Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}$. Perceived risk and realised loss are therefore distinct channels operating at different horizons, and the paper reports them separately.

A binary disaster $x_{t+1}\in\{0,1\}$ is drawn in period $t$. In the "business as usual" (BAU) state $x_{t+1} = 0$, sovereign bonds are repaid in full. In the disaster state $x_{t+1} = 1$, bondholders receive only a fraction $(1-\Delta^b)$ of the face value. The disaster will affect the sovereign bond haircut channel and destroy physical capital with the hypothesis that the disaster transmission runs through bond pricing, bank balance sheets, and through capital destruction as in \textcite{Gourio2012, Isore2017}. The consequences of this haircut for bank net worth and credit supply are discussed in Appendix \ref{sec:banking_block}. The disaster probability $\theta_t$ follows an exogenous log-AR(1) process specification as in \textcite{Isore2017}

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Cisaster_AR}
    \ln\theta_t = (1-\rho_\theta) \ln\bar\theta + \rho_\theta\ln\theta_{t-1} + \sigma_\theta\varepsilon_{\theta,t}, \qquad \varepsilon_{\theta,t} \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \tag{2.1.1}
  \mathcal{N}(0,1)
\end{equation}
%
where $\bar{\theta}$ is the unconditional mean disaster probability, $\rho_\theta \in (0,1)$ is persistence, and $\sigma_\theta > 0$ the standard deviation of log-innovations. The log-AR(1) specification ensures $\theta_t > 0$ for all realisations and prevents the probability
from reaching the boundary values $\{0, 1\}$. 


The disaster is a one-period event rather than an absorbing state. The indicator $x_t$ is drawn at the start of period $t$ from the probability set the previous period, $x_t\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\theta_{t-1})$; the innovation $\varepsilon_{\theta,t}$ then realises, $\theta_t$ becomes known, and production, trade and market clearing follow. The probability governing the \emph{next} period's draw is therefore $\theta_t=\Pr_t(x_{t+1}=1)$. Two properties of this timing are used repeatedly. First, $x_{t+1}$ is drawn independently of $\varepsilon_{\theta,t+1}$ conditional on time-$t$ information: this is what allows the disaster term to factor out of the certainty equivalent in~\eqref{eq:betatheta} and out of the bond Euler in Appendix~\ref{sec:Disaster_Transition}, and without it neither factorisation is exact. Second, a realisation $x_t=1$ does not raise the probability of $x_{t+1}=1$ beyond what~\eqref{eq:Cisaster_AR} implies, so the economy can enter and exit the disaster state; this distinguishes the specification from absorbing-state formulations such as \textcite{Brede2013}.

In the baseline specification, the productivity innovation is switched off, $\varepsilon_{z,t}=0$, so that the trend growth rate is deterministic
at $\mu$ and the only aggregate stochastic driver is the disaster probability $\theta_t$ (with the realised indicator $x_t$). This isolates the sovereign-disaster channel that is the object of study; a conventional total-factor-productivity shock can be reintroduced as a robustness exercise without altering the stationarised system, since $\varepsilon_{z,t}$ enters only through the growth factor $\Gamma_t=e^{x_t\ln(1-\Delta^k)+\mu+\varepsilon_{z,t}}$ already carried throughout Appendix~\ref{sec:Stationarization}.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $\theta_t$ is bounded and dimensionless; no detrending required. The process passes directly into the stationarized system in Appendix~B. $\to$ Appendix~B.

%The state probabilities $\theta_t(s)$ such that $\sum_{s\in S}\theta(s)=1$ are time-varying and universally known to all agents, upon which they make an investment decision at time $t$.

%Thus, if $x_{t+1} = 1$ in period $t+1$, the economy is not trapped, therefore $x_{t+2}$ will result in a fresh draw of the disaster state. The \textcite{Gourio2012} stationarization trick will analytically eliminate $x_{t+1}$ from the equilibrium system, replacing it with $\theta_t$ in the detrended equations, which will make the perturbation methods feasible despite the large nonlinearity.


\subsection{Households}\label{sec:households}

The economy is populated by a representative household who derives utility from consumption $C_t$ and leisure $1-L_t$, supplies labour to intermediate-goods firms, owns the monopolistically competitive firms and the capital-goods producer, deposits savings with commercial banks, and pays lump-sum taxes. The household maximises the following inter-temporal objective with Epstein-Zin recursive preferences:
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:EZW}
  \tilde{V}_t = \Biggl[
    \bigl[C_t(1-L_t)^{\varpi}\bigr]^{1-\psi}
    +\,\beta_0
    \Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[\tilde{V}_{t+1}^{1-\gamma}\bigr]\Bigr)^{\!
      \frac{1-\psi}{1-\gamma}}
  \Biggr]^{\!\frac{1}{1-\psi}} \tag{2.2.1}
\end{equation}
%
where $\beta_0\in(0,1)$ is the subjective discount factor, $\mathbb{E}_t$ the conditional expectations operator, $\psi>0$ the inverse of the intertemporal elasticity of substitution (\textsc{EIS}\,$=1/\psi$), $\gamma>0$ the coefficient of relative risk aversion (\textsc{rra}), and $\varpi>0$ the leisure weight in the consumption-leisure bundle.  Crucially, the Epstein-Zin formulation allows $\gamma\neq\psi$, decoupling risk aversion from the \textsc{EIS}; when $\gamma=\psi$ the recursion collapses to standard expected utility. 

The household's budget constraint is adapted from the standard disaster-risk literature.  Households own capital producers and deposit with commercial banks rather than holding sovereign bonds directly; accordingly, no bond-return term appears in the flow constraint, but the dividends of the capital goods producers enter as an additional income term

\begin{equation}\label{eq:budget}
C_t + T_t + D_t
\;=\;
w_t\,L_t + R^d_{t-1}\,D_{t-1} + \Pi_t^f + \Pi_t^{cp} + \Xi_t \tag{2.2.2}
\end{equation}
%
where $D_t$ are real bank deposits earning gross rate $R^d_t$ next period, $T_t$ lump-sum taxes, $w_t\equiv W_t/p_t$ the real wage, $\Pi_t^f \equiv Y_t - w_tL_t - r_t\tilde{K}_t$ dividends from monopolistically competitive firms, $\Pi_t^{cp}$ dividends from the capital-goods producer, and $\Xi_t$ the net transfer between households and the
two financially constrained sectors, whic is then the net worth returned by exiting entrepreneurs and bankers less the startup funds advanced to entrants, defined in Appendix~\ref{sec:banking_block}. Carrying $\Xi_t$ explicitly rather than treating it as negligible is what makes the aggregate resource constraint hold exactly.
%
The capital-goods producer is a competitive, household-owned pass-through but not preference-bearing agent with a technology of new installed capital delivered per unit final good is discounted by the adjustment cost, 

\begin{equation}\label{eq:CP_profit}
    \Pi_t^{cp} = Q_t \left[I_t - \frac{\tau}{2}K_t\left(\frac{I_t}{K_t} - \frac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)^2 \right] - I_t \tag{2.2.3}
\end{equation}
%
$K_t$ enters only as a scale variable (the aggregate installed capital stock, taken as given by the producer, and owned by entrepreneurs — see Appendix~\ref{sec:banking_block}). The producer is not preference-bearing and solves a static problem each period, so no Euler equation or new state variable is introduced. Maximising the capital good's producers profit over $I_t$ yields
%
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\partial {\Pi^{cp}}}{\partial I_t} = 0:\quad Q_t\left[1-\tau\left(\frac{I_t}{K_t}-\frac{\bar I}{\bar K}\right)\right]=1
\end{equation*}
%
which gives Tobin's $Q$:
%
\begin{equation}
  Q_t = \frac{1}{1-\tau\!\left(\dfrac{I_t}{K_t}-\dfrac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)}
  \tag{2.2.4}\label{eq:CP_TobinsQ}
\end{equation}
%
The household chooses the sequence $\{C_t,L_t,D_t\}_{t=0}^{\infty}$ to maximise utility \eqref{eq:EZW} subject to the budget constraint \eqref{eq:budget}, absent any direct sovereign-bond exposure.  First-order optimality conditions are derived in Appendix~\ref{AppendixA}. We obtain their final results as
%
\begin{align}
     \mathcal{M}_{t,t+1} &= \beta_0
      \left(\frac{C_{t+1}}{C_t}\right)^{\!-\psi}
      \!\!\left(\frac{1-L_{t+1}}{1-L_t}\right)^{\!\varpi(1-\psi)}
      \frac{V_{t+1}^{-\chi}}
           {\bigl(\mathbb{E}_t V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr)^{\frac{-\chi}{1-\chi}}} \tag{2.2.5}\label{eq:SDF} \\[6mm]
    C_t &= \frac{w_t(1-L_t)}{\varpi}\tag{2.2.6} \label{eq:HHO_C} \\[6mm]
  1 &= E_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}R_t^d] \tag{2.2.7}\label{eq:HHO_Deposit_Euler}
\end{align}
%
Following \textcite{Isore2017}, we integrate the disaster realisation $x_{t+1}$ out of the certainty equivalent $\mathcal{R}_t$ directly and using the Gourio (2012) scaling $\tilde V_{t+1}\big|_{x_{t+1}=1}=(1-\Delta^k)\,\tilde V_{t+1}\big|_{x_{t+1}=0}$ together with the symmetric productivity disaster\footnote{Productivity $z_t$ also contracts by $(1-\Delta^k)$ in the disaster state, following \textcite{Isore2017}.}, the binary indicator $x_{t+1}$ is eliminated through the \textcite{Gourio2012} trick in the detrended equilibrium system. The cost of this elimination is that the pseudo-discount factor $\beta(\theta_t)$ becomes time-varying: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betatheta}
    \beta(\theta_t)
    \;=\;
    \beta_0
    \Bigl[(1-\theta_t)+\theta_t(1-\Delta^k)^{1-\gamma}\Bigr]^{(1-\psi)/(1-\gamma)} \tag{2.2.8}
\end{equation}

At $\Delta^k=0$ or $\theta_t=0$ this reduces to $\beta_0$. For $\Delta^k>0$ and $\theta_t>0$, $\beta(\theta_t)$ varies with the disaster probability. When $\theta_t$ rises, the pseudo-discount factor shifts, producing an endogenous preference shock in the sense of \textcite{Isore2017}. The detrended Bellman, SDF, and capital-return equations (Appendix~\ref{sec:Stationarization}) carry $\beta(\theta_t)$ in place of $\beta_0$. This derivation is purely a property of the household's recursive preferences and is unaffected by which agent holds physical capital. The elimination applies to the certainty equivalent specifically. The indicator $x_{t+1}$ survives elsewhere in the household block, in particular in the trend factor $\Gamma_{t+1}$ carried by the stochastic discount factor itself and is integrated out separately wherever it appears; $\beta(\theta_t)$ is therefore not a universal substitute for the Gourio operation but the result of applying it at one specific exponent, $\kappa=1-\gamma$.
While \textcite{Isore2017} identify the risk-free rate through a household bond Euler $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\frac{(1+r_t)}{(1+\pi_{t+1})}]=1$, households in this model hold no sovereign bonds, so \eqref{eq:HHO_Deposit_Euler} replaces that equation on the household side. The risk-free rate is defined as
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riskfree_rate}
  R^f_t \;\equiv\; \frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]} \tag{2.2.9}
\end{equation}
%
from the SDF directly, independently of market clearing. As $R^d_t$ is known at $t$, \eqref{eq:HHO_Deposit_Euler} can be solved directly as $R^d_t=1/\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]$, which by definition \eqref{eq:riskfree_rate} is $R^f_t$. The equality $R^d_t=R^f_t$ is therefore an implication of the household's deposit Euler together with the definition of the risk-free rate, not a separate restriction. It is recorded in Appendix~\ref{secmain:marketclosing} for completeness but does not add an independent equation to the system.

The nominal policy rate $r_t$ set by the public authority's Taylor rule
(Section~\ref{secmain:public_authority}) is not the return on any asset
this household holds. \textcite{Isore2017} pin down the analogous object
directly through their own household's bond Euler, stated above; because
this household holds no bond of any kind, the same no-arbitrage
restriction is imposed here as the pricing condition a hypothetical,
zero-net-supply nominal bond would have to satisfy under the household
stochastic discount factor $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ of \eqref{eq:SDF}:

\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\,\frac{1+r_t}{1+\pi_{t+1}}\right] = 1
  \tag{2.2.10}
  \label{eq:A4_fisher}
\end{equation}

where $\pi_{t+1}\equiv p_{t+1}/p_t-1$. This does not collapse to the
commonly-quoted textbook form $R^f_t=(1+r_t)/\mathbb{E}_t[1+\pi_{t+1}]$
except under a zero-covariance condition between $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$
and $1+\pi_{t+1}$ that this model does not impose: the disaster
probability $\theta_t$ moves both the discount factor and expected
inflation simultaneously, so the two are generically correlated.
Equation~\eqref{eq:A4_fisher} is therefore carried in its exact form
through stationarization (Appendix~\ref{sec:Stationarization}, B.30);
the full derivation and the precise collapse condition are given in
Appendix~\ref{par:A17}.

Households save exclusively through bank deposits and banks never default in this model, $R^f_t$ as defined in~\eqref{eq:riskfree_rate} is a genuinely state-independent rate, so the return on a claim paying one unit in every realisation of $x_{t+1}$. This is a structural consequence of moving sovereign bonds onto bank balance sheets ($\phi>0$). While in \textcite{Isore2017}, the household's saving vehicle is the sovereign bond itself, which is state-contingent whenever $\Delta^b>0$; the object their bond Euler prices is therefore not the same object as~\eqref{eq:riskfree_rate}.  The consequence is that $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]$ must be evaluated explicitly rather than replaced by its no-disaster realisation, which is done in equation~\eqref{eq:B_EQ} below.

% We argue that introducing the capital goods producer and tehrefore moving the capital pricing rule from the household euler to an entrepreneur indifference is necessary. \textcite{Isore2017} impose a capital Euler $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}R^K_{t+1}]=1$ as a household optimality condition, pricing capital at the household's own risk-free discount rate; their $R^K_{t+1}$ combines the utilisation and investment-adjustment-cost margins into a single object, since their household chooses both jointly. In this paper, households hold no capital, so no such condition arises from the household's problem at all. Capital is instead priced by the entrepreneur's indifference condition $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]=R^S_t$ (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff}), which prices capital at the loan rate and added premium, reflecting the external finance premium entrepreneurs face. The technological definition of $R^K_{t+1}$ itself (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_RK}) decomposes cleanly into the utilisation margin (entrepreneur, Appendix~A.3.1) and the investment margin (capital-goods producer, equation~\eqref{eq:CP_TobinsQ} above) which were jointly determined by the household's combined FOC in \textcite{Isore2017}.
%
% The disaster does, however, propagate to the return on capital through the productivity channel.  The productivity process (Appendix~A.2, equation~\eqref{eq:prod_process_app}) retains the factor $e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\epsilon)}$ as a Stationarization device: in the detrended equilibrium system (Appendix~B), the rental rate $r^k_{t+1}$ inherits a disaster loading because $r^k_{t+1} \propto z_{t+1}^{1-\alpha}$ and $z_{t+1}$ is reduced by $(1-\epsilon)$ in a disaster.  Entrepreneurs therefore face a lower expected return on capital after a disaster even without physical destruction; the full derivation is in Appendix~A.2, equation~\eqref{eq:entr_ind}.



\subsection{Firms} \label{sec:firms_and_entrepreneurs}

The economy has a final-good producer and a continuum of intermediate-good producers. The final-good firm is perfectly competitive and aggregates
differentiated varieties into a single final good; the intermediate producers are monopolistically competitive, minimise costs given factor prices and then set prices subject to Calvo frictions. Calculations for each optimisation step can be found in Appendix~\ref{appsec:firms}


\subsubsection{Final Good Production}
 
Following \textcite{Isore2017} in the origins of \textcite{DixitStiglitz1977}, we assume that a representative final-goods firm aggregates intermediate varieties $Y_{j,t}$ into final output in perfect competition 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregator}
  Y_t = \left(\int_0^1 Y_{j,t}^{(\nu-1)/\nu}\,dj\right)^{\nu/(\nu-1)} \tag{2.3.1}
\end{equation}
%
The profit profit maximisation gives demand for variety $j$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Cemand}
  Y_{j,t} = Y_t\left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu} \tag{2.3.2}
\end{equation}

Zero profit for the aggregator then pins the price index at $p_t=\bigl(\int_0^1 p_{j,t}^{1-\nu}dj\bigr)^{1/(1-\nu)}$; the derivation is in
paragraph~\ref{appsec:finalgoodprod}.

\subsubsection{Intermediate Good Production} \label{sec:int_good}

The production of an intermediate good follows a two-step process. First, the firm minimizes costs to obtain aggregated real tems for wages and the rental rate of capital. Thenafter, firms are maximising their profits, subject to the demand schedule derived in the final good production, making the process backward-inducted. 


\paragraph{Cost Minimisation} Before final goods can be aggregated, intermediate good producers use labor and capital as production inputs. To minimise production costs, they choose optimal factor quantities in each given time period. Therefore, we assume that these firms take the wage $W_t$, the rental rate of capital $P_t^k$ as well as nominal factor prices as given. The underlying production function requires these firms produce at least as much of the intermediate good as it is demanded at a given price. The intra-temporal intermediate firm problem results in 

\begin{equation}\label{eq:cost_minimisation_int}
    \min_{L_{j,t}, \tilde{K}_{j,t}} W_{t}L_{j,t} + P_t^k\tilde{K}_{j,t} \qquad \text{s.t.} \quad \tilde{K}^\alpha_{j,t}(z_tL_{j,t})^{1-\alpha} \geq \left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}Y_t \tag{2.3.3}
\end{equation}
%
where $z_t$ denotes the level of productivity and $\tilde{K}_{j,t} \equiv u_tK_{j,t}$ expresses effective capital. Their first order solutions are derived in Appendix \ref{appsec:firms}. Hence, the aggregated real term optimality conditions are 
%
\begin{equation*}
    w_t = mc^*_t(1-\alpha)z_t^{1-\alpha}\tilde{K}_t^\alpha L_t^{-\alpha} \tag{2.3.4}
\end{equation*}
%
and
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:resale_price}
    P_t^{k, real} = mc^*_t\cdot\alpha\cdot z^{1-\alpha}\tilde{K}_t^{\alpha-1} L_t^{1-\alpha}   \tag{2.3.5}
\end{equation*}
%
As we further follow \textcite{Gourio2012} for the disaster process, we assume that productivity grows at 

\begin{equation} \label{eq:prod_process}
    \Gamma_t \equiv \frac{z_{t+1}}{z_t} = e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)+\mu+\epsilon_{z,t+1}} \tag{2.3.6}
\end{equation}
% 
\paragraph{Profit Maximisation}
We further assume price setting as in \textcite{Calvo1983}, in which each intermediate good producer may reset its price with probability $1-\zeta$ in any given period, while with  probability $\zeta$ it keeps its price unchanged. A firm that resets its price  $p_{j,t}$ at time $t$ chooses it to maximise the present discounted value of  profits over all future periods in which the price may remain fixed, subject to the demand schedule derived from \eqref{eq:aggregator} holds in every future period 
$t+s$ in which $p_{j,t}$ remains fixed:
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calvo_max}
    \max_{p_{j,t}}\; \mathbb{E}_t \sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s\, Q_{t, t+s}\,
    \frac{p_{j,t}Y_{j,t+s} - W_{t+s}L_{j,t+s} - P^k_{t+s}\tilde{K}_{j,t+s}}{p_{t+s}}
    \tag{2.3.7}
\end{equation}
%
where $Q_{t,t+s}\equiv\prod_{i=1}^{s}Q_{t+i-1,t+i}$ is the household's real stochastic discount factor inherited from \eqref{eq:SDF}, with
$Q_{t,t}=1$. Since $Q_{t,t+s}$ prices real payoffs, nominal profits are deflated by $p_{t+s}$.
%
The ratio between recent price and current price required to derive the inflation rate ratio is derived in a maximisation Appendix \ref{appsec:profitmax}. Defining the gross reset inflation rate as $1+\pi_t^* \equiv p_t^*/p_{t-1}$  and the gross inflation rate as $1+\pi_t \equiv p_t/p_{t-1}$, such that $p_t^*/p_t = (1+\pi_t^*)/(1+\pi_t)$, the ratio above can be written recursively by introducing auxiliary variables $\Xi_{1_t}$ and $\Xi_{2_t}$:
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reset_inflation}\tag{2.3.8}
    1 + \pi_t^* = \frac{\nu}{\nu-1}\frac{\Xi_{1_t}}{\Xi_{2_t}}(1+\pi_t)
\end{equation}
%
where the recursive auxiliary variables are defined as
%
\begin{align}
    \Xi_{1_t} &= Y_t mc_t^* + \zeta\, E_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}
    (1+\pi_{t+1})^\nu \,\Xi_{1_{t+1}}\right] \label{eq:aux1}\tag{2.3.9}\\[4pt]
    \Xi_{2_t} &= Y_t + \zeta\, E_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}
    (1+\pi_{t+1})^{\nu-1}\,\Xi_{2_{t+1}}\right] \label{eq:aux2}\tag{2.3.10}
\end{align}
%
with $mc_t^*$ the real marginal cost of production at time $t$. The aggregated price index of the economy consists of a share of firms $1-\zeta$ that are not able to reset their pricing levels at given time $t$ and its remainder fraction $\zeta$ of the previous period price expressed as

\begin{equation*}
    p_t^{1-\nu}= (1-\zeta)p_t^{*1-\nu} + \zeta p_{t-1}^{1-\nu}  = \int_0^{1-\zeta} p_t^{*1-\nu}dj + \int_{1-\zeta}^1 p_{j,t-1}^{1-\nu}
\end{equation*}
%
so the inflation rate is expressed as 

\begin{equation}\label{eq:inflation_rate}
    (1+\pi_t)^{1-\nu} = (1-\zeta)(1+\pi_t^*)^{1-\nu} + \zeta \tag{2.3.11}
\end{equation}
Sticky prices leave the cross-section of relative prices dispersed. Collecting that dispersion in $\Omega_t\equiv\int_0^1(p_{j,t}/p_t)^{-\nu}dj\geq1$, which obeys its own Calvo recursion (paragraph~\ref{appsec:agginflation}), the aggregate production function is
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:agg_production}
  Y_t=\frac{\tilde{K}_t^{\alpha}(z_tL_t)^{1-\alpha}}{\Omega_t}
  \tag{2.3.12}
\end{equation}
%
with $\Omega_t=1$ only when every firm charges $p_t$.

\subsection{Entrepreneurs and Banking Sector}

This section extends the developments of \textcite{Isore2017} by integrating an entrepreneur à la \textcite{Bernanke1999} into a NK DSGE setting with financial frictions, which connects the household and firm sector with the banking sector developed from \textcite{Gertler2011}. 

\subsubsection{Entrepreneurs}

The economy consists of a continuum of identical, risk-neutral entrepreneurs of mass one. Each purchase $K_{t+1}$ units of installed capital at the end of period~$t$ from the capital goods producer (Appendix~\ref{appsec:households}) at price $Q_t$ and rents capital to intermediate goods firms in period $t=1$ at the real rental rate $P^k_{t+1}$. Capital that is retained at the same period is valued at the same price $Q_t$ that applies to newly purchased units. The framing of buying and selling capital used throughout this section is a standard accounting convention in this literature and not a literal transaction. A continuing entrepreneur need not physically dispose of capital it retains, since resale and purchase occur at the same price. Each entrepreneur has net worth $N^e_t$ and finances the gap $Q_tK_{t+1} - N^e_t$ by borrowing from a bank at gross real loan rate $R^S_t$, contracted at end of period~$t$.

Following the costly-state-verification framework of \textcite{Townsend1979} and \textcite{Bernanke1999}, the optimal debt contract generates a reduced-form external finance premium, taken as given below. The underlying contract-theory is derived in Appendix~\ref{sec:banking_block}. $K_{t+1}$ here denotes the quantity committed to at the end of period $t$, before $x_{t+1}$ is reaslized while the capital law of motion's $K_{t+1}$ denotes the  the same object after the disaster realization, related by the Gourio factor.
Given capital $K_{t+1}$ acquired at $t$, the entrepreneur chooses period-$(t{+}1)$ utilisation $u_{t+1}$ to maximise gross revenue from the capital stock. Doing so takes as given the rental price $P^{k,real}_{t+1}$ as derived in the intermediate good producers cost minimisation resulting in equation~\eqref{eq:resale_price} and the resale price $Q_{t+1}$ set by the capital-goods producer (Equation~\ref{eq:CP_TobinsQ}). Since the disaster factor $e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)}$ and the capital stock $K_{t+1}$ scale total revenue multiplicatively and do not depend on $u_{t+1}$, maximising realised revenue (for any realisation of $x_{t+1}$ and any idiosyncratic draw $\omega_{j,t+1}$) is equivalent to maximising per-unit revenue:
%
\begin{equation*}
  \max_{u_{t+1}}\;\;   P_{t+1}^{k, real}\,u_{t+1} + Q_{t+1}\bigl(1-\delta_0 u_{t+1}^{\eta}\bigr)
\end{equation*}
%
This objective is well-defined independently of the debt contract's default threshold $\bar\omega_t$. Therefore, utilisation maximises the total surplus available for division between entrepreneur and bank, not the entrepreneur's own share of it, so the choice is unaffected by contract details. The first-order condition is derived as 
%
\begin{equation}
  P_{t+1}^{k, real}  = Q_{t+1}\,\delta_0\,\eta\,u_{t+1}^{\eta-1}
  \tag{2.4.1}\label{eq:A3_util}
\end{equation}
%
with second-order condition $-Q_{t+1}\delta_0\eta(\eta-1)u_{t+1}^{\eta-2}<0$, showing that the standard convexity assumption on the depreciation function is satisfied for $\eta>1$ which has been already imposed throughout the model. 

\paragraph{Consistency with the Capital-Goods Producer.}
The two margins that \textcite{Isore2017}'s household jointly optimises are now split across two agents, but both continue to price the same market-clearing capital good. The capital-goods producer's investment condition~\eqref{eq:CP_TobinsQ}, initiated at date $t+1$, and the
entrepreneur's utilisation condition~\eqref{eq:A3_util} both determine the single date-$(t{+}1)$ price $Q_{t+1}$:
%
\begin{equation*}
  Q_{t+1}
  \;=\;\frac{1}{1-\tau\!\left(\dfrac{I_{t+1}}{K_{t+1}}-\dfrac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)}
  \;=\;\frac{rt^k_{t+1}}{\delta_0\,\eta\,u_{t+1}^{\eta-1}}.
\end{equation*}
%
Eliminating $Q_{t+1}$ between the two recovers exactly \textcite{Isore2017}'s single combined household condition, which is the same restriction that \textcite{Isore2017} obtain as a single household condition, the two margins there being solved jointly rather than by separate agents. The full derivation is in Appendix~\ref{subsec:entrepreneurs}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Return on Capital}
\label{par:return_on_capital}
 
Evaluated at the optimal utilisation \eqref{eq:A3_util}, the gross real return to holding one unit of capital from $t$ to $t+1$ is
 
\begin{equation}
  R^K_{t+1}
  = e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)}\,
    \frac{P^{k, real}_{t+1}\, u_{t+1}
          + Q_{t+1}\bigl(1 - \delta_0\, u_{t+1}^{\eta}\bigr)}
         {Q_t} \tag{2.4.2}
  \label{eq:A3_RK}
\end{equation}
%
where $u_{t+1}$ solves \eqref{eq:A3_util} and $\delta_{t+1} = \delta_0\, u_{t+1}^{\eta}$ is the resulting utilisation-dependent depreciation rate.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $R^K_{t+1}$ is a ratio of detrended prices and is already scale-invariant. No further detrending required. $\to$ Appendix~B.


% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{External Finance Premium.}
\label{par:A313}

The CSV contracting problem of \textcite{Bernanke1999} yields a premium
schedule $f(\cdot)$ that is decreasing in the entrepreneur's equity share, but
the model is closed there in log-linear form and no level functional form is
stated. \textcite{Gelain2010} likewise works with the log-linearised
relationship
%
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{s}_t \;=\; -\,\chi^e\bigl(\hat{n}^e_t - \hat{q}_t - \hat{k}^n_{t+1}\bigr),
  \qquad
  \hat{s}_t \equiv \mathbb{E}_t\hat{r}^k_{t+1}-\hat{r}_t,
\end{equation*}
%
in which $\chi^e$ is the elasticity of the external finance premium with respect
to the leverage ratio and is the object estimated. Because the model here is
solved by third-order perturbation, a log-linear elasticity is not sufficient:
the second and third derivatives of $f(\cdot)$ enter the solution, so a level
form must be specified. We adopt the isoelastic schedule
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A3_EFP}
  f\!\left(\frac{N^e_t}{Q_t K^n_{t+1}}\right)
  \;\equiv\; f_0
    \left(\frac{N^e_t}{Q_t K^n_{t+1}}\right)^{\!-\chi^e},
  \qquad \chi^e > 0,\; f_0 > 0 \tag{2.4.3}
\end{equation}
%
which is the unique level form with constant elasticity $\chi^e$, and therefore
the unique form whose log-linearisation reproduces the relationship above
exactly at every point rather than only at the calibration point. It inherits
the sign properties of the \textcite{Bernanke1999} schedule: $f'<0$, so a fall
in $N^e_t$ widens the premium and amplifies the initial shock through the
financial accelerator. The choice is nonetheless an extrapolation of a locally
estimated elasticity to a global functional form, and the higher-order terms it
implies are not separately identified by the estimate; this is recorded as a
limitation in Section~\ref{sec:limitation}.

The parameter $f_0$ is a normalisation, not an independent structural
parameter: it is back-solved so that the schedule reproduces a target
steady-state entrepreneur premium $\mathbb{E}[\bar R^K]/\bar R^S$ at the target
equity ratio $\varkappa \equiv \bar N^e/(\bar Q\bar K^{n})$, giving
$f_0=\mathcal{F}\,\varkappa^{\chi^e}$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:D7}). The bank margin
$\bar R^S/\bar R^d$ is a separate calibration target, matched through the
leverage multiplier $\lambda$ rather than through $f_0$.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $N^e_t / Q_t K_{t+1}$ is a ratio of real quantities both growing at trend rate $Z_t$; the ratio is stationary after detrending by $Z_t$. Full stationarized form in Appendix~B. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Entrepreneur Net Worth Accumulation.}
\label{par:A314}

Following further \textcite{Bernanke1999}, a fraction $\sigma^e \in (0,1)$ of entrepreneurs survive each period and carry their net worth forward. The exiting fraction $(1-\sigma^e)$ transfers net worth to households as a lump sum. A startup transfer $\iota^e_t\equiv\iota^ez_t$ from households to entering entrepreneurs prevents
the entrepreneurs net worth to tend towards a value of zero:

\begin{equation}
  N^e_t
  = \sigma^e \!\left[
      \underbrace{R^K_t \cdot Q_{t-1}K_t}_{\text{gross return on capital}}
      - R^S_{t-1} \cdot \underbrace{\bigl(Q_{t-1}K_t - N^e_{t-1}\bigr)}_{\text{interest on externally financed capital}}
    \right]
    + \iota_t^e \tag{2.4.4}
  \label{eq:A3_NE}
\end{equation}
% 
In the standard \textcite{Bernanke1999} model without a banking sector, the lender's opportunity cost is the risk-free rate, so equation~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} places the premium between $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]$ and $R^f_t$ directly.  Here the lender is a constrained bank whose required return on the loan book is $R^S_t>R^d_t$, determined by the binding leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323} together with loan-market clearing (A.5.4).  The premium of~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} then applies on top of $R^S_t$ rather than on top of $R^f_t$, which is the sense in which the two frictions stack.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $R^K_t \cdot Q_{t-1}K_t$ has dimension $Z_{t-1}$. Divide through by $Z_{t-1}$ and apply the Gourio-trick growth normalization. Full stationarized form: $\hat{n}^e_t = \sigma^e e^{-\mu}[\cdots] + \hat{\iota}^e$. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Entrepreneur Balance Sheet Identity}
\label{par:A315}

Bank lending to entrepreneurs, valued at the capital price, equals the funding gap between the capital purchased and the entrepreneur's own net worth:
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A3_entrep_BS}
  Q_tS_t \;\equiv\; Q_t K^n_{t+1} - N^e_t \tag{2.4.5}
\end{equation}

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Divide through by $Z_t$. All three terms detrend directly. $\to$ Appendix~B.


% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Capital Demand: Free-Entry Condition}
\label{par:capital_market_clearing}
 
Financing of $K_{t+1}$ is decided at the end of period $t$, taking $Q_t$ (capital-goods producer, Appendix~\ref{appsec:households}), the loan rate $R^S_t$, and the premium schedule~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} as given.  An individual entrepreneur is atomistic relative to the continuum and therefore treats both $R^S_t$ and the aggregate equity ratio entering $f(\cdot)$ parametrically: it does not perceive its own $K_{t+1}$ choice as moving either.  Because expected profit is linear in $K_{t+1}$, a determinate equilibrium requires the coefficient on $K_{t+1}$ to vanish — otherwise the atomistic entrepreneur would want an unbounded or zero capital stock (Appendix, full argument). Following \textcite{Gertler2011}, free entry and competitive credit markets impose exactly that:

%
% A.3.1.6 -- the participation / free-entry condition, where f(.) enters:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[R^K_{t+1}\right]
  = f\!\left(\frac{N_t^e}{Q_tK_{t+1}}\right) R^S_t
  \label{eq:A3_indiff} \tag{2.4.6}
\end{equation}
\textcite{Isore2017} impose the household capital Euler $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}] = R^f_t$ directly. In this model, entrepreneurs finance capital with bank loans, so the relevant no-arbitrage condition is against the loan rate, with $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}] > R^S_t \geq R^d_t = R^f_t$. Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} replaces their capital Euler and is the equation that connects the firm sector \ref{appsec:firms} to the banking sector \ref{sec:banksov}. 

The rate wedge is the external finance premium derived in equation~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP}. Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} is a condition that $R^S_t$ must satisfy; it does not by itself determine $R^S_t$.  The equilibrium loan rate is pinned by the balance sheet chain rather than by ~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} itself. Given the development of bank net worth $N^b_t$, the binding leverage constraint and the home-bias identity in the next subsection, loan supply is fixed at $Q_tS_t=(1-\phi)\lambda_t N^b_t$. The entrepreneur balance sheet~\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS} then fixes $Q_tK_{t+1}=Q_tS_t+N^e_t$ and hence the equity ratio entering $f(\cdot)$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} returns the $R^S_t$ consistent with that quantity. Loan-market clearing~\eqref{eq:loanmarketclearing} is the statement that these two routes to $Q_tS_t$ agree; it is a consequence of the optimality conditions, not an addition to them. This is the channel through which a sovereign loss on bank balance sheets raises the cost of credit to entrepreneurs.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Both sides are gross rates --- scale-invariant. $\to$ Appendix~B.
 
% \medskip
% \noindent\textit{Dynare implementation:} \texttt{RL = RK(+1)}.


\subsubsection{Banks with Sovereign Bonds}
\label{sec:banksov}

A continuum of identical banks of mass one each raise deposits $D_{j,t}$ from households at gross real deposit rate $R^d_t$, accumulate net worth
$N^b_{j,t}$ from retained earnings, and hold two asset classes: loans $L_{j,t}$ to entrepreneurs at gross real loan rate $R^S_t$, and domestic sovereign bonds $B^b_{j,t}$ earning gross real return $R^b_t$. The portfolio share of sovereign bonds in total bank assets is fixed by the home-bias parameter $\phi \in [0,1)$.

Banks are owned by households. To motivate an endogenous constraint on bank leverage, \textcite{Gertler2011} introduce a moral hazard friction: at the start of each period, a banker can divert a fraction $\theta^b$ of managed assets and transfer them to the owning household as private gain. Depositors lend only if the continuation value of operating exceeds the gain from diverting. This incentive compatibility condition becomes the leverage constraint in equation~\eqref{eq:A323}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Bank Balance Sheet Identity}
\label{par:A321}

\begin{equation}
  \underbrace{\,Q_t S_{j,t}}_{\text{loans}}
  + \underbrace{\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}}_{\text{sovereign bonds}}
  =
  \underbrace{N^b_{j,t}}_{\text{net worth}}
  + \underbrace{D_{j,t}}_{\text{deposits}} \tag{2.4.7}
  \label{eq:A3_bankBS}
\end{equation}
%
The home-bias parameter $\phi$ splits bank assets between loans and sovereign bonds. Setting $\phi=0$ removes sovereign exposure from bank balance sheets while
leaving every other friction in place: the \textcite{Gertler2011} leverage
constraint and the \textcite{Bernanke1999} external finance premium both
survive, and capital continues to be intermediated rather than held directly by households. The $\phi=0$ economy is therefore not \textcite{Isore2017}'s model, which it would nest only if the two domestic financial frictions were switched off as well. It reproduces their disaster-and-capital block inside a model with financial intermediation, which is what makes it the right benchmark: the comparison isolates the marginal contribution of sovereign exposure rather than the contribution of financial frictions as such.
 
\begin{equation}
    \,Q_t S_{j,t}+Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}
    \;\leq\;
    \lambda_t\,N^b_{j,t}
  \tag{2.4.8}
  \label{eq:A323}
\end{equation}
% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Home-Bias Portfolio Identity}
\label{par:A324}

Domestic banks are the predominant holders of domestic sovereign debt in the euro area, reflecting well-documented regulatory and structural incentives\parencite{Gennaioli2014,Bocola2016}.

The parameter $\phi \in [0,1)$ captures this as a structural characteristic of the bank balance sheet rather than a portfolio decision variable. Given these institutional frictions banks are effectively price-takers in the sovereign bond market at the composition margin, and $\phi$ is calibrated to the observed average domestic sovereign bond share in euro-area bank total assets. Formally, $\phi$ is the bond share of total assets $A_{j,t}\equiv Q_tS_{j,t}+Q^b_tB^b_{j,t}$, giving the portfolio condition
%
\begin{equation}
  Q^b_t B^b_t \;=\; \frac{\phi}{1-\phi}\,Q_tS_t
  \tag{2.4.9}\label{eq:A3_homebias}
\end{equation}
%
Combined with the entrepreneur balance sheet~\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS}, equation~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias} closes the bank asset side as $Q_tS_t$ is pinned by entrepreneur equity and the binding leverage constraint \eqref{eq:A323}, and $Q^b_t B^b_t$ is then determined proportionally. The counterfactual sets $\phi = 0$, which eliminates the sovereign bond portfolio to the standard \textcite{Gertler2011} specification with loans only, shutting down the sovereign-bank transmission channel while preserving the standard BGG financial accelerator.

\paragraph{Bank Net Worth Accumulation.}
\label{par:A325}

Aggregating across surviving bankers $\sigma^b$ and adding startup transfers $\iota_t^b$ to newly entering bankers, the \textcite{Gertler2011} augmented bank net worth accumulation equation reads as

\begin{equation}
  N^b_t
  = \sigma^b
    \Bigl[
      (R^S_{t-1} - R^d_{t-1})\,Q_{t-1}S_{t-1}
      + (R^b_t - R^d_{t-1})\,\,Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}
      + R^d_{t-1}\,N^b_{t-1}
    \Bigr]
    + \iota_t^b \tag{2.4.10}
  \label{eq:Bank_Net_worth}
\end{equation}
%
Surviving bankers earn at first the excess return on loans $(R^S_{t-1} - R^d_{t-1})$ on their loan portfolio, secondly the excess return on sovereign bonds $(R^b_t - R^d_{t-1})$ on their bond portfolio and thirdly the deposit rate on their existing net worth. The startup transfer $\iota$ is calibrated residually following \textcite{Christiano2014} to match a target $\bar{N}^b/\bar{K}$ ratio.

% When $\theta_t$ rises, $Q^b_t$ falls (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}), which reduces the mark-to-market value $Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}$ and compresses $R^b_t$ (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_Rb}). The second term in brackets in~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} therefore falls, eroding $N^b_t$ without any realised defaults, purely through the anticipated sovereign haircut. This is the sovereign-bank channel.

\subsubsection{Disaster Transmission Mechanism}

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Equilibrium Bond Pricing Condition}
\label{par:bank_bond:euler}

% Clarify if it is the banks or household who decide here
% This Equation should be written out as a maximisation problem to detremine who makes the decision 

% Is \phi a decision parameter or is phi an outcome of the bank - supply of public bonds is exogenous and price is exogenous (maybe think about that phi is an optimal decision of bansk (opt 1) secon: banks have no choice, please gov buy necause no other nationalities didnt buy them - !!!adjustment should be the price and not share!!!) Because if banks have no choice

% Idea: Adding haircut \delta to capital equation to have \phi = 0 and \phi = 1 possibility 

With portfolio composition pinned by the structural parameter $\phi$ \eqref{eq:A323}, sovereign bonds are not a free choice variable for the individual bank: the quantity $Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}$ is determined residually once total assets $A_{j,t} = \lambda_t N^b_{j,t}$ and loan volume $Q_t S_{j,t}$ are fixed by the binding leverage constraint (\ref{eq:A323}). The sovereign bond price $Q^b_t$ is therefore an equilibrium price that adjusts endogenously to clear the bond market at the fixed composition ratio $\phi$.

The bond is therefore priced by an unconstrained marginal investor who discounts at the household stochastic discount factor $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ of~\eqref{eq:SDF}:
%
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\, R^b_{t+1}\right] = 1
  \tag{2.4.11}
  \label{eq:A3_bondeuler}
\end{equation}
%
A bank whose leverage constraint binds values an extra unit of any asset above its household-SDF price, so \eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} is an assumption about who the marginal holder is, not an implication of the bank's problem. It is the pricing-side counterpart of treating $\phi$ as a balance-sheet characteristic rather than a portfolio choice. The two remarks that make this precise are in paragraph~\ref{par:bank_bond:euler}.

Since banks are owned by households, they discount at the household stochastic discount factor $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ of \eqref{eq:SDF}, so let
%
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal{M}^d_{t,t+1}\equiv\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[M_{t+1}\mid x_{t+1}=1\right],
  \qquad
\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}\equiv\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[M_{t+1}\mid x_{t+1}=0\right]
\end{equation*}
%
denote its disaster- and no-disaster-state values, for which in our model only $\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}$ will be relevant, as no disaster occurs.  


While in \textcite{Isore2017} bonds are held by households and therefore the household optimality condition incorporates sovereign bonds, we assume for the sake of isolating the sovereign-bank transmission channel cleanly that households do not hold sovereign bonds. Their budget constraint contains no bond term (see \ref{appsec:households}). Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} is therefore derived from the bank's equilibrium pricing requirement, not the household's optimality condition. The pricing implication is identical because the bank uses the household SDF $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ as its discount factor.

Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} is the equilibrium bond pricing condition for which $Q^b_t$ adjusts endogenously until it holds. It takes the standard asset pricing form $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1} R^i_{t+1}] = 1$ for asset~$i$, identical in structure to \textcite{Isore2017} but derived from the bank's willingness-to-hold condition rather than a portfolio first-order condition.

\paragraph{Asset-Specific Resilience.}
\label{par:asset_specific_resilience}
 
Following \textcite{Gabaix2012}, the linearity-generating process assigns each risky asset an asset-specific resilience that summarizes the disaster-state expected return loss. For the sovereign bond paying $(1 - \Delta^b)$ in the disaster state and $1$ in the BAU state we can state that
 
\begin{equation}
  H^b_t \;=\; 1 - \theta_t \cdot \Delta^b \cdot \tilde\Lambda^M_t
  \tag{2.4.12}
  \label{eq:A3_resil}
\end{equation}
%
where $\Delta^b \in (0,1)$ is the sovereign haircut in a disaster and
%
\begin{equation*}
  \tilde\Lambda^M_t
  \;\equiv\;
  \frac{\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}^d_{t,t+1}\right]}
       {\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}\right]}
\end{equation*}
%
is the disaster SDF loading, normalised by the conditional mean of the stochastic discount factor rather than by its no-disaster realisation.  The normalisation is what makes $H^b_t$ commensurate with the risk-free rate $R^f_t=1/\mathbb{E}_t[M_{t+1}]$ against which it is divided in~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}; without it the bond Euler~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} would hold only to first order in $\theta_t$.  $H^b_t$ captures the disaster-probability-weighted reduction in the bond's risk-adjusted payoff. Substituting the bond return into the Euler equation and applying the \textcite{Gabaix2012} decomposition gives the closed-form bond price:

\begin{equation}
   Q^b_t \;=\; \frac{H^b_t}{R^f_t}
   \;=\; \frac{1-\theta_t\,\Delta^b\,\tilde\Lambda^M_t}{R^f_t}
   \tag{2.4.13}
   \label{eq:A3_bondprice}
\end{equation}
%
The decomposition is derived in Appendix~\ref{sec:Disaster_Transition}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Sovereign Bond Gross Return}
\label{par:A334}
 
The one-period gross real return on the sovereign bond, accounting
for the disaster haircut:
 
\begin{equation}
  R^b_{t+1}
  = \frac{1 - x_{t+1}\,\Delta^b}{Q^b_t}
  \tag{2.4.14}
  \label{eq:A3_Rb}
\end{equation}
%
where $x_{t+1} \in \{0,1\}$ is the disaster realisation at the
start of period $t+1$, with $R^b_{t+1} = 1/Q^b_t$ in the BAU state and $R^b_{t+1} = (1-\Delta^b)/Q^b_t < 1/Q^b_t$ in the disaster state.
%  
Taking the conditional expectation of equation~\eqref{eq:A3_Rb} and substituting
equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}, we arrive at the expected sovereign bond gross return:

\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[R^b_{t+1}\right]
  = \frac{1 - \theta_t\,\Delta^b}{Q^b_t}
  = \frac{(1 - \theta_t\,\Delta^b)\,R^f_t}
         {1 - \theta_t\,\Delta^b\,\tilde\Lambda_t^M}
  \tag{2.4.15}
  \label{eq:A3_ERb}
\end{equation}
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $R^b_{t+1}$ is a gross rate --- scale-invariant. $\to$ Appendix~B.
The sovereign spread is defined as the excess of the bond's promised gross yield over the risk-free rate,
%
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{spread}_t
  \;\equiv\; \frac{1}{Q^b_t} - R^f_t
  \;=\; R^f_t\,\frac{\theta_t\,\Delta^b\,\tilde\Lambda^M_t}{H^b_t}
  \;\approx\; \theta_t \cdot \Delta^b \cdot \tilde\Lambda^M_t
  \tag{2.4.16}
  \label{eq:A3_spread}
\end{equation}
%
We report the promised-yield spread $1/Q^b_t - R^f_t$ throughout, since it is the object recorded in observed sovereign spreads and against which $\Delta^b$ is calibrated (see Appendix~\ref{sec:Disaster_Transition} for its relationship to the expected-return spread).

Disaster risk reaches real activity through two routes that must be kept distinct. The first is a pure preference-and-pricing effect: a rise in $\theta_t$ shifts the pseudo-discount factor $\beta(\theta_t)$ of~\eqref{eq:betatheta}, moves the risk-free rate, and raises the sovereign spread of~\eqref{eq:A3_spread} on impact. This route requires no default ever to occur, but it does \emph{not} operate through bank balance sheets: net worth\ accumulation~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} applies the realised return $R^b_t$ to the \emph{predetermined} position $Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}$, so a change in $\theta_t$ leaves $N^b_t$ unaffected at $t$. The second route is the sovereign--bank channel proper and requires a realised disaster, $x_t=1$: the haircut $\Delta^b$ lowers $R^b_t$, erodes $N^b_t$ through~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth}, tightens the leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323}, contracts loan supply, and raises the external finance premium of~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} through the fall in the entrepreneur equity  ratio. Only the second route is amplified by $\phi$, and it is the one the $\phi=0$ counterfactual switches off. With one-period bonds no mark-to-market channel is available: bank asset values are not revalued by current-period news about $\theta_t$.

\paragraph{Immediate channel: perceived risk via the bank's incentive constraint.}
 
A rise in $\theta_t$ tightens the sovereign bond's resilience $H^b_t$ (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_resil}) and, through it, the current bond price $Q^b_t$, before any default is realised. Because $R^b_{t+1}=1/Q^b_t$, this immediately revises the bank's expectation of next period's bond return, and the marginal values of net worth and of assets in the banker's value function $\nu^b_t,\eta^b_t$ are themselves conditional expectations over exactly this object. The leverage multiplier $\lambda_t=\nu_t/(\theta^b-\eta_t)$ therefore moves in the same period as the shock, tightening or loosening permitted balance-sheet size before bank net worth $N^b_t$ has changed at all, A $N^b_t$ as predetermined by~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} and cannot respond within the period. This channel is thus active for any $\theta_t$ innovation, realised or not, and its sign depends on how the shock moves the bank's portfolio margin $(1-\phi)R^S_t+\phi\mathbb{E}_t[R^b_{t+1}]-R^d_t$ relative to the risk-free rate, not mechanically on the direction of $\theta_t$ alone.


\paragraph{Lagged channel: realised default via bank net worth.}
 
The second channel requires an actual default and transmits with a one-period lag, through the mark-to-market loss on bonds already held. The complete propagation from a realized sovereign default to real activity, with each arrow citing the equation responsible:
 
\begin{align*}
  &x_t=1 \;\text{(realised sovereign default)}
  \;\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:A3_Rb}}\;
    R^b_t = \frac{1-\Delta^b}{Q^b_{t-1}}\downarrow
  \;\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth}}\;
    N^b_t\downarrow \;\text{(bank net worth)} \\[15pt]
  &\;\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:A323}\,\&\,\eqref{eq:A3_homebias}}\;
    Q_tS_t \downarrow \;\text{(loan supply)}
  \;\xrightarrow{\text{\eqref{eq:loanmarketclearing}}}\;
    \bigl(Q_t K_{t+1} - N^e_t\bigr)\downarrow,\quad
    R^S_t\uparrow \;\text{(bank margin)} \\[15pt]
  &\;\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:A3_NE}}\;
    N^e_t \downarrow
  \;\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:A3_EFP}}\;
    \bigl(\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}] - R^S_t\bigr)\uparrow \;\text{(entrepreneur margin)}
  \;\Rightarrow\;
    I_t,\,Y_t,\,C_t \downarrow \;\text{recession}
\end{align*}

The two channels are not competing descriptions of the same mechanism but genuinely separate margins, distinguished by what they require and how quickly they act. The immediate channel operates through the bank's forward-looking portfolio choice and requires only a change in $\theta_t$; it is present even along a sample path on which $x_t=1$ never realizes, and its magnitude is quantified in Section~\ref{sec:results}. The lagged channel operates through realized losses on bonds already on the balance sheet and requires $x_t=1$ to be activated. This channel is silent for a structural reason, as the realised returns $R^b_t$ and $R^K_t$ entering the model's recursions are both coded at their disaster-free ($x=0$) branch by construction, so only the disaster probability $\theta_t$, not an actual default, propagates through the system under perturbation methods. For simulation of a disaster path, the realised return terms would require a rederivation with a realised disaster term and a solution method that admits discrete draws, as proposed for the long-term bond extension (Appendix~\ref{sec:LongTermBond}).

\subsection{Public Authority}\label{secmain:public_authority}


Following \textcite{Isore2017}, the public authority issues one-period sovereign bonds, collects lump-sum taxes from households, and sets the nominal interest rate via a Taylor-type rule. No government expenditure on goods enters the model and fiscal policy is reduced to the financing of outstanding debt through taxes and new bond issuance.
 
The government issues a quantity $B_t$ of one-period nominal bonds $B_{t+1}$ at the end of period $t$. Outstanding bonds $B_t$ carry nominal gross interest rate $(1 + r_{t-1})$. In the disaster state $x_t = 1$, a fraction $\Delta^b$ of the face value is not repaid (partial sovereign default); in the BAU state ($x_t = 0$) full repayment is made. The flow budget constraint of the government is:
 
\begin{equation}
  T_t = R^b_t Q^b_{t-1} B^b_{t-1} - Q^b_t B^b_t 
  \tag{2.5.1}
  \label{eq:A4_govBC}
\end{equation}
%
where  $T_t$ are real lump-sum taxes. The budget is balanced every period, as tax revenues plus new bond issuance exactly cover the cost of outstanding debt service net of any disaster haircut. This eliminates public savings as a state variable and implies that taxes are entirely endogenous, adjusting to satisfy~\eqref{eq:A4_govBC} each period.
 
Following \textcite{Isore2017}'s own public-authority block, the public authority sets the nominal interest rate $r_t$ according to a
Taylor-type rule that targets the deviations of inflation from its steady-state value $\bar{\pi}$ and of detrended output from its
steady-state level $\bar{Y}$; its relationship to the real risk-free rate $R^f_t$ of Section~\ref{sec:households} is established there,
following IS2017's own placement of the analogous condition in the household's problem rather than the public authority's:

\begin{equation}
  r_t = \rho_r\, r_{t-1}
        + (1 - \rho_r)\!\left[
            \varphi_\pi(\pi_t - \bar{\pi})
            + \varphi_Y\!\left(\frac{Y_t}{z_t} - \bar{Y}\right)
            + \bar{r}
          \right]
  \tag{2.5.2}
  \label{eq:A5_taylor}
\end{equation}

where $\rho_r \in (0,1)$ is the interest rate smoothing parameter,
$\varphi_\pi > 1$ the inflation response coefficient (Taylor principle),
$\varphi_Y > 0$ the output-gap response coefficient, and $\bar{r}$ the
steady-state nominal rate. The disaster probability $\theta_t$ does not
enter the Taylor rule directly; it affects $r_t$ only through its
general-equilibrium effects on $\pi_t$ and $Y_t$.

We implement these measures as standard New Keynesian elements, even though actual enforcement in the economy is not common sense. Whether consumers actually have a sound understanding and incorporate both principles has been studied by \textcite{Draeger2016}, which find that about 52\% build expectations which are in line with the Fisher equation, while 46\% include the Taylor rule relation in forming their expectations.

% \parencite[Eq.~11, Appendix~A.3, Eq.~A.21]{Isore2017} 
 
% \noindent\textit{Steady-state note:} At the non-stochastic steady state, $r_{t-1} = r_t = \bar{r}$ and $\pi_t = \bar{\pi}$, $Y_t/z_t = \bar{Y}$. The rule is trivially satisfied. $\bar{r}$ is pinned by the Fisher equation at steady state: $1 + \bar{r} = \bar{R}^f \cdot (1 + \bar{\pi})$.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} The Taylor rule is stated in terms of the nominal rate $r_t$ and inflation $\pi_t$ (both stationary) and detrended output $Y_t/z_t$ (stationary by construction). No further detrending required. $\to$ Appendix~B.

\subsection{Market Clearing}\label{secmain:marketclosing}

Market clearing conditions impose the requirement that all markets clear simultaneously in equilibrium. Together with the optimality conditions from A.1--A.4, that is the case if the goods and labour market is cleared. Subsequently, we will derive the good market and reinstate the labour, capital services, sovereign bond and loan market clearing conditions.  
% 
\paragraph{Good Market} Every financial claim in this model (loans, deposits, sovereign bonds, entrepreneur and bank net worth) is held by one agent and owed by another. This means that trading or accumulating these claims reallocates resources already counted once and neither creates nor destroys them. The aggregate resource constraint follows from going back only the flows that connect the financial layer to real production and consumption. By the firm's income identity, the right-hand side equals aggregate output:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:goodclearing}
Y_t=C_t+I_t \tag{2.6.1}
\end{equation}

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Labour and Capital Services Market}
\label{par:A512}
 
Aggregate demand for labour and capital services from intermediate goods firms equals aggregate labour supply from households. Integrating the cost-minimisation condition across intermediate firms $j \in [0,1]$:
 
\begin{align}
  \int_0^1 L_{j,t}\,dj = L_t 
  \tag{2.6.2}
    \label{eq:A5_labour} \\[4mm]
  \int_0^1 \tilde{K}_{j,t}\,dj = \tilde{K}_t 
  \tag{2.6.3}
\end{align}
%
These conditions are already used in the cost minimisation process during the derivation of the aggregate production function \eqref{eq:cost_minimisation_int} where it eliminates the firm-level labour demand from the aggregate resource constraint. At the equilibrium level, they ensure that the real wage $W^{\text{real}}_t$ and the real rental rate $P^{k,\text{real}}_t$ adjust to clear the labour and capital services market. 
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Hours $L_{j,t}$ are stationary (no trend); condition holds unchanged in the detrended system. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $\tilde{K}_{j,t}$ inherits the trend of $K_t$; divide through by $Z_t$. $\to$ Appendix~B.
 
% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Sovereign Bond Market}
\label{par:A513}
Sovereign bond market clearing follows directly from the home-bias identity~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias}, combined with the institutional fact that banks are the sole domestic holders of government debt, $B_t^b \equiv B_t$. 

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Divide through by $z_t$. Both $Q^b_t B^b_t$ and $Q_tS_t$ grow at trend $z_t$; the ratio $\phi/(1-\phi)$ is dimensionless. Detrended form is identical. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Loan Market}
\label{par:loan_clearing}
The quantity $Q_tS_t$ appears on both sides of the credit market. From the entrepreneur's balance sheet~\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS}, $Q_tS_t = Q_tK_{t+1}-N^e_t$ is loan \emph{demand}, determined by the capital entrepreneurs wish to finance net of their own net worth. From the aggregated binding leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323} together with the home-bias identity~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias}, $Q_tS_t = (1-\phi)\lambda_t N^b_t$ is loan \emph{supply}, determined by bank net worth and the leverage limit. Their equality,
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:loanmarketclearing}
  Q_tK_{t+1}-N^e_t \;=\; (1-\phi)\,\lambda_t\,N^b_t, \tag{2.6.4}
\end{equation}
%
is the loan market clearing condition. It is not an independent structural equation but the requirement that entrepreneur credit demand and bank credit supply coincide; equivalently, it pins the equilibrium loan rate $R^S_t$ at which the entrepreneur participation condition~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} holds. As with deposit and sovereign bond market clearing, it is a consequence of the agents' optimality conditions rather than an addition to them.


 
\section{Calibration}\label{secmain:calibrationandresults}

In this paper, we decide to calibrate targeted moments following \textcite{Isore2017} on disaster risk variables, utility function values and further for the capital adjustment cost and calvo-price stickiness parameter, while most production parameters and public authority targets come from \textcite{Taylor1993, Coenen2018}. Further, we need to newly fit parameters related to the banking sector to financial market data. Several of \textcite{Isore2017} targets are themselves drawn from the broader macro-finance literature. Therefore, Table \ref{tab:calibration_one} shows the calibration values with their sourcing from \textcite{Isore2017}. They take their baseline values for capital and productivity destruction from \textcite{Barro2008}, and the persistence of log-disaster risk from \textcite{Gourio2012}. The standard deviation of disaster-risk innovation has been derived by themselves to match empirical evidence. Further, utility parameters have relied solely on \textcite{Gourio2012} as the valuation of leisure goes along with the Epstein-Zin preferences utility function form. Regarding the degree of risk aversion, also taken from \textcite{Gourio2012}, \textcite{Isore2017} note that \textcite{Barro2008} report a mean estimate near 3, with a 95\% confidence interval of 2 to 4. For the discount factor $\beta_0$, we follow \textcite{Bernanke1999} and \textcite{Gertler2011} in setting $\beta_0 = 0.9985$. However, this is of minor relevance, as \textcite{Isore2017} finds that this parameter plays no material role for the results obtained, \textbf{which we can confirm. }

For the elasticity of intertemporal substitution, we follow \textcite{Isore2017} in setting the EIS  to 0.5 rather than the value of 2 used by \textcite{Gourio2012}. The two calibrations belong to different traditions as far as we can identify. First, there are models targeting asset-pricing moments to match the level, volatility, and cyclicality of the equity premium, which is often exactly 2 as in \textcite{Barro2008, Gourio2012}, but requires generally an EIS above unity. Second, the broader macro-consumption literature instead finds values below unity, with \textcite{Havranek2015}'s meta-analysis of 2{,}735 estimates across 169 studies reporting a mean close to 0.5. Since this paper targets macroeconomic and credit-market outcomes rather than asset-pricing moments the rationale for the higher value does not apply, and we adopt $\mathrm{EIS}=0.5$ accordingly, exactly as \textcite{Isore2017} do for the same reason. \textcite{Isore2017} themselves show the two calibrations produce qualitatively identical, sign-mirrored dynamics, so the choice is not first-order for the model's qualitative predictions, but it is the natural default given this paper's focus. For the remaining standard Keynesian parameters, we replace \textcite{Isore2017}'s literature values with those calibrated in the ECB's NAWM~II model (\textcite{Coenen2018}) and borrow the Taylor-rule weight parameters from \textcite{Taylor1993} directly. 

For the banking, entrepreneur and sovereign block that we integrate, all targets need discussion as we newly source them. The sovereign haircut $\Delta^b=0.37$ matches \textcite{Cruces2013}'s documented average haircut across 180 sovereign debt restructurings between 1970 and 2010. To determine the actual home bias value $\phi$, we calculate the home-bias parameter by using the  product of total sovereign bond holdings to total assets ($\approx$9.3\%) and the share of domestic bonds within that total ($\approx$28\%) based on ECB supervisory data for a sample of 317 euro area banks \parencite{Buch2026}. In a period between December 2014 and December 2025, this value has been between 1.7\% and 3.3\% in the Euro-area, with a more recent reading of around 2.5\%. Therefore $\phi$ is set to 0.03, matching euro area banks' current domestic sovereign bond exposure as a share of total assets. For the banker survival rate $\sigma^b$, we follow \textcite{Coenen2018} again, whose wholesale banks are subject to the same Gertler-Karadi leverage constraint used in this thesis. NAWM II's wholesale  banks are the natural counterpart to our banking sector, as they are the layer bearing the leverage constraint. For the values of EFP elasticity $\chi^e$ and the entrepreneur survival rate $\sigma^e$, we use the posterior mean estimates and the calibration parameter for the entrepreneur leverage rate by \textcite{Gelain2010}, who estimates a model with the external finance premium for the euro-area. Therefore, we adopt the same fixed steady-state entrepreneur target originating in \textcite{Bernanke1999}. To our knowledge, there is no empirical paper that gives a value for its decomposition into numerical values for the entrepreneur premium and bank loan spread. In this absence of a euro-area estimate for a combined BGG-GK specification, we set the aggregate premium in a share of 120bp/80bp between the entrepreneur and bank frictions as the yearly target to be consistent with the euro-area's approximative 200 bp single friction benchmark from \textcite{Gelain2010}, which is the bank-only spread in their paper alone. Table \ref{tab:calibration_frictions} lists the non-policy parameters for our model.

%%%%%% Calibration

Our calibration is limited in regards to a decomposition of the external finance premium target, as no direct euro-area re-estimate of the split-fraction exists. The closest structural analogue is \textcite{Rannenberg2016} which is, however, US-calibrated. 

%%%%%% Long-term Disaster Bond
While the model has been proven to show the firing of the disaster risk anticipation channel, we were not yet able to implement such a version that the disaster actually realizes. Further contributions should look into the developments of New Keynesian Models with financial frictions that incorporate Epstein-Zin preferences with disaster risk and a scenario in which the disaster actually materializes. 

Additionally, the existing model could be extended through implementing a long-term bond structure. We modelled a basic component of such along the development of this paper, however, this bond structure could not have been succesfully included until the submission of this work for the following reasons: first, the equation incorporates a disaster term $x_{t+1}$ explicitly, following

\begin{equation}
R^b_{t+1} = \frac{(1-x_{t+1}\Deltaᵇ)(1+\rho_\beta Q^b_{t+1})}{Q^b_t}   
\end{equation}

Under standard perturbation, the disaster term still has to be zero everywhere in any AR(1)-probability-shock GIRF, for the identical reason as the one-period bond, to actually make the lagged channel work. In addition to this long-term bond, one needs a deterministic MIT-shock experiment which forces the default at a chosen date. This would require a second, different solution method layered on top of the extension itself. Next to solving for $x_t$ exogenously, the long-duration bond's new state variable must be rederived for every equation touching $\frac{Q_bB}{Q_b}$, including Appendix C's closed-form steady state, which is currently derived for the one-period bond specifically.

Thus, we only present the component to be included in our future work in Appendix \ref{sec:LongTermBond}.

% -------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Households}\label{appsec:households}
% -------------------------------------------------------------------
% ======================================================================
%  APPENDIX A  ——  Household Optimality Conditions
%  (full Lagrangian + labeled FOCs)
% ======================================================================
Households have Epstein--Zin--Weil preferences
%
\begin{equation*}
  \tilde{V}_t = \Biggl[
    \bigl[C_t(1-L_t)^{\varpi}\bigr]^{1-\psi}
    +\,\beta_0
    \Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[\tilde{V}_{t+1}^{1-\gamma}\bigr]\Bigr)^{\!
      \frac{1-\psi}{1-\gamma}}
  \Biggr]^{\!\frac{1}{1-\psi}}
\end{equation*}
%
%
Raising both sides to the power $1-\psi$ and defining $V_t\equiv\tilde V_t^{\,1-\psi}$ together with $\chi\equiv 1-\frac{1-\gamma}{1-\psi}$, so that $1-\chi=\frac{1-\gamma}{1-\psi}$ and $\tilde V_{t+1}^{\,1-\gamma}=V_{t+1}^{\,\frac{1-\gamma}{1-\psi}}=V_{t+1}^{\,1-\chi}$:
%
\begin{align*}
  V_t
  &= \bigl[C_t(1-L_t)^{\varpi}\bigr]^{1-\psi}
     +\beta_0\Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[\tilde V_{t+1}^{1-\gamma}\bigr]\Bigr)^{\!\frac{1-\psi}{1-\gamma}}\\[2mm]
  &= \bigl[C_t(1-L_t)^{\varpi}\bigr]^{1-\psi}
     +\beta_0\Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr]\Bigr)^{\!\frac{1}{1-\chi}}
\end{align*}
where the original Epstein-Zin objective is \eqref{eq:EZW}.


In \textcite{Isore2017}, households hold sovereign bonds directly and the bond return is haircut by $e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^b)}$ in a disaster. Households there also choose utilisation and investment, holding physical capital directly. In this paper, households do not hold sovereign bonds directly as they deposit with banks, who hold bonds on their balance sheets (home bias $\phi$). They likewise do not hold physical capital directly as capital is purchased and held by entrepreneurs, financed by entrepreneur net worth and bank loans (Appendix~\ref{sec:banking_block}). Instead, households own a competitive capital-goods-producer sector and receive its profit $\Pi_t^{cp}$ as a dividend, alongside dividends $\Pi_t^f$ from monopolistically competitive firms.
%
\begin{equation*}
 C_t + T_t + D_t
\;=\;
W^{\mathrm{real}}_t\,L_t 
 + R^d_{t-1}\,D_{t-1} + \Pi_t^f+ \Pi_t^{cp}
\end{equation*}
%

\paragraph{Lagrangian.}
Define the certainty equivalent
\begin{equation*}\label{eq:CE}
  \mathcal{R}_t \;\equiv\;
  \Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr]\Bigr)^{\!\frac{1}{1-\chi}}
\end{equation*}
so that the Bellman equation for \eqref{eq:EZW} reads $V_t = [C_t(1-L_t)^{\varpi}]^{1-\psi}+\beta_0\,\mathcal{R}_t$. Attaching multiplier $\Lambda^H_t\geq 0$ to budget constraint \eqref{eq:budget}:
%

\begin{align*}
\mathcal{L}_t \;=\;&\; V_t \\
&-\;\Lambda^H_t\!\left[C_t+T_t+D_t
  -W^{\mathrm{real}}_t L_t
  -R^d_{t-1}D_{t-1}-\Pi_t^f-\Pi_t^{cp}\right]
\end{align*}
%
Unlike \textcite{Isore2017}'s household, which additionally holds physical capital and chooses $\{I_t,u_t,K_{t+1}\}$ subject to a capital-accumulation constraint, the household's problem here contains no capital-related choice variable and no capital-accumulation constraint. Consequently there is no multiplier capital-accumulation multiplier $\Lambda^C_t$. The investment, utilisation, and capital-return margins that $\Lambda^C_t$ would otherwise generate are instead derived from the capital-goods producer's and entrepreneur's own optimisation problems in section \ref{sec:banking_block}.
%
\paragraph{First-Order Conditions.} Attach the multiplier $\Lambda^H_t\geq 0$ to the household budget constraint \eqref{eq:budget}. Because the continuation value $V_{t+1}$ depends on the deposit stock $D_t$ carried into $t+1$, the problem is solved recursively: the static conditions for $C_t$ and $L_t$ follow from differentiating $\mathcal{L}_t$ directly, while the condition for $D_t$ requires differentiating the certainty equivalent $\mathcal{R}_t$ through $V_{t+1}$ and closing with the envelope condition.
%
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_t}{\partial C_t} = 0: &\quad
  \Lambda^H_t \;=\; (1-\psi)\,C_t^{-\psi}\,(1-L_t)^{\varpi(1-\psi)}
\end{align*}
%
so that the growth rate of the multiplier is
%
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{\Lambda^H_{t+1}}{\Lambda^H_t}
  \;=\;\left(\frac{C_{t+1}}{C_t}\right)^{\!-\psi}
       \left(\frac{1-L_{t+1}}{1-L_t}\right)^{\!\varpi(1-\psi)}
\end{equation*}

The partial derivative of $V_t$ with respect to $L_t$ satisfies $\partial V_t/\partial L_t = -(\varpi C_t)/(1-L_t)\cdot\Lambda_t^B$; combining with the labour-income term in the budget constraint yields the standard intratemporal labour-leisure condition:
%
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial L_t} = 0:&\quad
  \frac{1-L_t}{C_t} = \frac{\varpi}{W^{\mathrm{real}}_t} \notag
\end{align*}

Differentiating with respect to $D_t$, the continuation value enters only through $\mathcal{R}_t$. Applying the chain rule to
$\mathcal{R}_t=(\mathbb{E}_t[V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}])^{1/(1-\chi)}$ and using $\tfrac{1}{1-\chi}-1=\tfrac{\chi}{1-\chi}$,
%
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_t}{\partial D_t} = 0: &\quad
  \Lambda^H_t \;=\; \beta_0\,
  \Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr]\Bigr)^{\!\frac{\chi}{1-\chi}}
  \,\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[V_{t+1}^{-\chi}\,
    \frac{\partial V_{t+1}}{\partial D_t}\right]
\end{align*}
%
The envelope condition for the period-$(t+1)$ problem, in which $D_t$ appears only through the term $R^d_t D_t$ of the budget constraint, gives $\partial V_{t+1}/\partial D_t = \Lambda^H_{t+1}R^d_t$. Substituting and dividing through by $\Lambda^H_t$,
%
\begin{equation*}
  1 \;=\; \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[
    \beta_0\,\frac{\Lambda^H_{t+1}}{\Lambda^B_t}\,
    \frac{V_{t+1}^{-\chi}}
         {\bigl(\mathbb{E}_t V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr)^{\frac{-\chi}{1-\chi}}}
    \,R^d_t\right]
\end{equation*}
%
which defines the Epstein--Zin stochastic discount factor
%
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\;\equiv\;\beta_0\,\frac{\Lambda^H_{t+1}}{\Lambda^H_t}\,
  \frac{V_{t+1}^{-\chi}}
       {\bigl(\mathbb{E}_t V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr)^{\frac{-\chi}{1-\chi}}}
\end{equation*}
%
and hence
%
\begin{align*}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\,R^d_t\right] = 1
\end{align*}
%
Since $C_t$, $L_t$ and $D_t$ enter neither the capital-accumulation constraint nor the return on capital, the three conditions above take the same form as in \textcite{Isore2017}; what is removed is the additional set of conditions for $\{I_t,u_t,K_{t+1}\}$ and the associated envelope terms in $\Lambda^C_t$.

We obtain the household's optimality conditions as

%
\begin{align}
     \mathcal{M}_{t,t+1} &= \beta_0
      \left(\frac{C_{t+1}}{C_t}\right)^{\!-\psi}
      \!\!\left(\frac{1-L_{t+1}}{1-L_t}\right)^{\!\varpi(1-\psi)}
      \frac{V_{t+1}^{-\chi}}
           {\bigl(\mathbb{E}_t V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr)^{\frac{-\chi}{1-\chi}}} \label{app:SDF} \tag{A.1.1} \\[6mm]
    C_t &= \frac{w_t(1-L_t)}{\varpi} \tag{A.1.2}\\[6mm]
  1 &= E_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}R_t^d] \tag{A.1.3}
\end{align}
%
% Equation~\eqref{eq:SDF} defines $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ state by state as a random variable measurable at $t+1$, not its conditional mean. The disaster indicator $x_{t+1}$ enters $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ through the growth factor $\Gamma_{t+1}$, and different equilibrium conditions require different moments of the resulting distribution: the deposit Euler~\eqref{eq:HHO_Deposit_Euler} and the risk-free rate~\eqref{eq:riskfree_rate} require $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]$, whereas any condition discounting a payoff that grows with the trend, like the Bellman recursion and the Calvo auxiliaries of Appendix~\ref{appsec:firms}, requires $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\Gamma_{t+1}]$.  Because $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ and $\Gamma_{t+1}$ are both functions of the same indicator $x_{t+1}$, these two moments are not related by $\mathbb{E}_t[Q\Gamma]=\mathbb{E}_t[Q]\,\mathbb{E}_t[\Gamma]$.  They are evaluated separately in Appendix~\ref{sec:Stationarization}.

\paragraph{Endogenous Discount Factor $\beta(\theta_t)$.}
Following \textcite{Isore2017}, we integrate the disaster realisation $x_{t+1}$ out of the certainty equivalent $\mathcal{R}_t$ directly and using the Gourio (2012) scaling $\tilde V_{t+1}\big|_{x_{t+1}=1}=(1-\Delta^k)\,\tilde V_{t+1}\big|_{x_{t+1}=0}$ together with the symmetric productivity disaster\footnote{Productivity $z_t$ also contracts by $(1-\Delta^k)$ in the disaster state, following \textcite{Isore2017}.}, the binary indicator $x_{t+1}$ is eliminated through the \textcite{Gourio2012} trick in the detrended equilibrium system. Writing the no-disaster continuation value as
$\tilde V^{\,nd}_{t+1}\equiv \tilde V_{t+1}\big|_{x_{t+1}=0}$, the Gourio scaling reads $\tilde V_{t+1}=\tilde V^{\,nd}_{t+1}(1-\Delta^k)^{x_{t+1}}$.
Since $x_{t+1}$ is a Bernoulli draw with $\Pr_t(x_{t+1}=1)=\theta_t$ that is independent of $\tilde V^{\,nd}_{t+1}$ conditional on time-$t$ information,
%
\begin{align*}
  \mathbb{E}_t\bigl[V_{t+1}^{1-\chi}\bigr]
  &= \mathbb{E}_t\bigl[\tilde V_{t+1}^{1-\gamma}\bigr]
   = \mathbb{E}_t\Bigl[\bigl(\tilde V^{\,nd}_{t+1}\bigr)^{1-\gamma}
       (1-\Delta^k)^{(1-\gamma)x_{t+1}}\Bigr] \\[2mm]
  &= \Bigl[(1-\theta_t)+\theta_t(1-\Delta^k)^{1-\gamma}\Bigr]\,
     \mathbb{E}_t\Bigl[\bigl(\tilde V^{\,nd}_{t+1}\bigr)^{1-\gamma}\Bigr]
\end{align*}
%
Raising to the power $\tfrac{1}{1-\chi}=\tfrac{1-\psi}{1-\gamma}$ and multiplying by $\beta_0$, the disaster term factors out of the certainty equivalent as a deterministic function of $\theta_t$:
%
\begin{equation*}
  \beta_0\,\mathcal{R}_t
  \;=\;\beta(\theta_t)\,
  \Bigl(\mathbb{E}_t\bigl[\bigl(\tilde V^{\,nd}_{t+1}\bigr)^{1-\gamma}\bigr]
  \Bigr)^{\!\frac{1-\psi}{1-\gamma}} \tag{A.1.4}
\end{equation*}
%
with
\begin{equation}
    \beta(\theta_t)
    \;=\;
    \beta_0
    \Bigl[(1-\theta_t)+\theta_t(1-\Delta^k)^{1-\gamma}\Bigr]^{(1-\psi)/(1-\gamma)} \tag{A.1.5}
\end{equation}

At $\Delta^k=0$ or $\theta_t=0$ this reduces to $\beta_0$. For $\Delta^k>0$ and $\theta_t>0$, $\beta(\theta_t)$ varies with the disaster probability. When $\theta_t$ rises, the pseudo-discount factor shifts, producing an endogenous preference shock in the sense of \textcite{Isore2017}. The detrended Bellman, SDF, and capital-return equations (Appendix~\ref{sec:Stationarization}) carry $\beta(\theta_t)$ in place of $\beta_0$. This derivation is purely a property of the household's recursive preferences and is unaffected by which agent holds physical capital. The elimination applies to the certainty equivalent specifically. The indicator $x_{t+1}$ survives elsewhere in the household block, in particular in the trend factor $\Gamma_{t+1}$ carried by the stochastic discount factor itself and is integrated out separately wherever it appears; $\beta(\theta_t)$ is therefore not a universal substitute for the Gourio operation but the result of applying it at one specific exponent, $\kappa=1-\gamma$.

\paragraph{Risk-Free Rate and Deposit Market.}
