While \textcite{Isore2017} identify the risk-free rate through a household bond Euler $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\frac{(1+r_t)}{(1+\pi_{t+1})}]=1$, households in this model hold no sovereign bonds, so \eqref{eq:HHO_Deposit_Euler} replaces that equation on the household side. The risk-free rate is defined as
%
\begin{equation*}
  R^f_t \;\equiv\; \frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]} \tag{A.1.6}
\end{equation*}
%
from the SDF directly, independently of market clearing. Because $R^d_t$ is known at $t$, \eqref{eq:HHO_Deposit_Euler} can be solved
directly as $R^d_t=1/\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]$, which by definition \eqref{eq:riskfree_rate} is $R^f_t$. The equality $R^d_t=R^f_t$ is therefore an
implication of the household's deposit Euler together with the definition of the risk-free rate, not a separate restriction; it is recorded in Appendix~\ref{secmain:marketclosing} for completeness but does not add an independent equation to the system.

Because households save exclusively through bank deposits and banks never default in this model, $R^f_t$ as defined in~\eqref{eq:riskfree_rate} is a genuinely state-independent rate, so the return on a claim paying one unit in every realisation of $x_{t+1}$. This is a structural consequence of moving sovereign bonds onto bank balance sheets ($\phi>0$). While in \textcite{Isore2017}, the household's saving vehicle is the sovereign bond itself, which is state-contingent whenever $\Delta^b>0$; the object their bond Euler prices is therefore not the same object as~\eqref{eq:riskfree_rate}.  The consequence is that $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]$ must be evaluated explicitly rather than replaced by its no-disaster realisation, which is done in equation~\eqref{eq:B_EQ} below.

\paragraph{Fisher Equation} \label{par:A17}  The nominal policy rate $r_t$ set by the Taylor rule of Appendix~\ref{sec:public} is not the price of any asset an optimising household or bank actually holds in this model — households save only through deposits, and banks hold only loans and sovereign bonds (\ref{sec:banksov}). \textcite{Isore2017} pin down the analogous object directly, through their own household's bond Euler $\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}(1+r_t)/(1+\pi_{t+1})]=1$, since their household holds that bond. This model's household holds no bond of any kind, so the same relationship is imposed here as a no-arbitrage restriction rather than a household optimality condition — exactly parallel to how the sovereign bond price $Q^b_t$ is pinned down in \S\ref{sec:Disaster_Transition} by requiring the household SDF to correctly price an asset the household does not literally hold.  Consider a hypothetical one-period nominal bond in zero net supply, priced at $1$ in period $t$ and paying $(1+r_t)$ with certainty, in nominal terms, in period $t+1$. Its real payoff is $(1+r_t)/(1+\pi_{t+1})$, random because $\pi_{t+1}\equiv p_{t+1}/p_t-1$ is not known at $t$. For the market in this notional asset to clear at zero net holdings while every household remains individually optimising — i.e. for no household to have a strict incentive to buy or sell it — its price must equal the value the household's own stochastic discount factor $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ of \eqref{eq:SDF} assigns to its real payoff:  

\begin{equation}   
\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\,\frac{1+r_t}{1+\pi_{t+1}}\right] = 1   \tag{A.1.7} 
\end{equation} 

Because $(1+r_t)$ is known at $t$, it factors out of the expectation:  

\begin{equation*}   1+r_t \;=\; \frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}/(1+\pi_{t+1})\right]} 
\end{equation*}  

\paragraph{Fisher Equation Covariance Condition} Write $X\equiv\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ and $Y\equiv 1/(1+\pi_{t+1})$, and use the covariance identity $\mathbb{E}_t[XY]=\mathbb{E}_t[X]\,\mathbb{E}_t[Y]+\mathrm{Cov}_t(X,Y)$:  \begin{equation*}   \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\frac{\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}}{1+\pi_{t+1}}\right]   = \mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\frac{1}{1+\pi_{t+1}}\right]   + \mathrm{Cov}_t\!\left(\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1},\,\frac{1}{1+\pi_{t+1}}\right) \end{equation*}  Using $R^f_t\equiv 1/\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]$ (A.1.6), equation~\eqref{eq:A4_fisher} reduces to the commonly-quoted approximate form $R^f_t=(1+r_t)/\mathbb{E}_t[1+\pi_{t+1}]$ if and only if two conditions both hold: (i) $\mathrm{Cov}_t\bigl(\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1},\, 1/(1+\pi_{t+1})\bigr)=0$, and (ii) $\mathbb{E}_t[1/(1+\pi_{t+1})]=1/\mathbb{E}_t[1+\pi_{t+1}]$ — the latter itself an equality only up to a Jensen's-inequality gap that vanishes exclusively when $\pi_{t+1}$ is deterministic.  Neither condition holds in this model. Condition (i) fails because $\theta_t$ is the model's sole aggregate risk factor and moves both objects at once: a rise in perceived disaster risk simultaneously raises $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$'s exposure to the bad state through the Epstein-Zin risk adjustment of \eqref{app:SDF}, and shifts expected inflation through the New Keynesian block's own response to $\theta_t$, so $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ and $\pi_{t+1}$ are generically correlated conditional on $\theta_t$. Condition (ii) fails whenever $\pi_{t+1}$ has any conditional variance at all, which it does at every date given the shock $\varepsilon_{\theta,t+1}$. Both gaps are second-order-and-above objects in the innovations, so they are invisible to a first-order (linearised) solution but not to the third-order solution this thesis targets — which is precisely why equation~\eqref{eq:A4_fisher} is carried in its exact form into the stationarised system (\S\ref{sec:Stationarization}, B.30) rather than replaced by a log-linear substitute: doing so would reintroduce, at exactly this one equation, the first-order approximation the rest of the solution method is built to avoid.  The policy rate $r_t$ is not the return on the defaultable sovereign bond. The latter is $R^b_{t+1}$ of \eqref{eq:A3_Rb}, which carries the haircut $\Delta^b$; the wedge between its promised yield $1/Q^b_t$ and $R^f_t$ is the sovereign spread of \eqref{eq:A3_spread}.


\paragraph{Capital-Goods Producer.}\label{par:CP}
A representative, perfectly competitive, household-owned capital-goods producer converts final-good investment into installed capital using the adjustment-cost technology $\mathcal{S}(I_t/K_t)$ where $\mathcal{S}(x)\equiv x-\frac{\tau}{2}\bigl(x-\bar I/\bar K\bigr)^2$ denotes the investment adjustment-cost technology, with $\mathcal{S}(\bar I/\bar K)=\bar I/\bar K$ and $\mathcal{S}'(\bar I/\bar K)=1$. They purchase $I_t$ units of the final good at unit price and selling the resulting capital at the market price $Q_t$. Its static per-period profit, rebated lump-sum to the household as $\Pi^{cp}_t$, is
%
\begin{equation*}
  \Pi_t^{cp} = Q_t\left[I_t - \frac{\tau}{2}K_t\left(\frac{I_t}{K_t} - \frac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)^2\right] - I_t
  \;=\; Q_t\,\!\left(\frac{I_t}{K_t}\right)K_t - I_t
\end{equation*}
%
$K_t$ enters only as a scale variable (the aggregate installed capital stock, taken as given by the producer, and owned by entrepreneurs — see Appendix~\ref{sec:banking_block}). The producer is not preference-bearing and solves a static problem each period, so no Euler equation or new state variable is introduced. Maximising the capital good's producers profit over $I_t$ yields
%
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\partial {\Pi^{cp}}}{\partial I_t} = 0:\quad Q_t\left[1-\tau\left(\frac{I_t}{K_t}-\frac{\bar I}{\bar K}\right)\right]=1
\end{equation*}
%
which gives Tobin's $Q$:
%
\begin{equation*}
  Q_t = \frac{1}{1-\tau\!\left(\dfrac{I_t}{K_t}-\dfrac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)}
\end{equation*}
%
identical in form to \textcite{Isore2017}'s household-derived Tobin's-Q relation, since the underlying adjustment-cost technology is unchanged. Only the agent solving the maximisation problem has moved from the household's joint utilisation-investment margin to the capital-goods producer's own problem to be consistent with the argument that household owned entrepreneurs make capital and investment decisions. At the steady state $I/K=\bar I/\bar K$ gives $\bar Q=1$ and $\overline{\Pi^{cp}}=0$, so the producer earns zero profit along the balanced growth path, as a competitive technology with no rents should. Away from the steady state, writing $g_t\equiv I_t/K_t-\bar I/\bar K$ and using $Q_t-1=\tau g_t/(1-\tau g_t)$,
%
\begin{equation*}
  \Pi_t^{cp}=(Q_t-1)I_t-Q_t\frac{\tau}{2}K_tg_t^2
  =\frac{\tau g_t}{1-\tau g_t}\,I_t-\frac{\tau}{2}\,\frac{K_tg_t^{2}}{1-\tau g_t}
\end{equation*}
%
which is first-order in the investment gap and vanishes only at $g_t=0$. It is therefore carried explicitly in the household budget constraint. It does not, however, enter the aggregate resource constraint: $I_t$ is real final-good use regardless of how the transaction between producer and entrepreneur nets out privately (see Appendix~\ref{secmain:marketclosing}).

% -------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Firms}\label{appsec:firms}
% -------------------------------------------------------------------
The economy has a final-good producer and a continuum of intermediate-good producers. The final-good firm is monopolistically competitive and aggregates
differentiated varieties into a single final good; the intermediate producers are monopolistically competitive, minimise costs given factor prices and then set prices subject to Calvo frictions.


\subsubsection{Final Good Production}\label{appsec:finalgoodprod}
 
\paragraph{Final Good Production Aggregation}
Following \textcite{Isore2017} in the origins of \textcite{DixitStiglitz1977}, we assume that a representative final-goods firm aggregates intermediate varieties $Y_{j,t}$ into final output in perfect competition 
\begin{equation}
  Y_t = \left(\int_0^1 Y_{j,t}^{(\nu-1)/\nu}\,dj\right)^{\nu/(\nu-1)} \tag{A.2.1}
\end{equation}
%
The firm maximises its final aggregated goods given by 
%
\begin{equation*}
    \max_{Y_{j,t}} \quad p_t\left(\int_0^1Y_{j,t}^{(\frac{\nu-1}{\nu})}\right)^{\frac{\nu}{\nu-1}} - \int_0^1 p_{j,t}Y_{j,t} dj
\end{equation*}
%
Taking the first-order condition with respect to $Y_{j,t}$ and applying the chain rule:
%
\begin{equation*}
    p_t \cdot \frac{\nu}{\nu-1} \left(\int_0^1 Y_{j,t}^{\frac{\nu-1}{\nu}}\,dj\right)^{\frac{\nu}{\nu-1}-1} \cdot \frac{\nu-1}{\nu}\, Y_{j,t}^{\frac{\nu-1}{\nu}-1} = p_{j,t}
\end{equation*}
%
Simplifying the exponent of the integral, noting that $\frac{\nu}{\nu-1} - 1 = \frac{1}{\nu-1}$, and the exponent on $Y_{j,t}$, noting that $\frac{\nu-1}{\nu} - 1 = -\frac{1}{\nu}$:
%
\begin{equation*}
    p_t \left(\int_0^1 Y_{j,t}^{\frac{\nu-1}{\nu}}\,dj\right)^{\frac{1}{\nu-1}} Y_{j,t}^{-\frac{1}{\nu}} = p_{j,t}
\end{equation*}
%
Recognising that $\left(\int_0^1 Y_{j,t}^{\frac{\nu-1}{\nu}}\,dj\right)^{\frac{1}{\nu-1}} = \left(\int_0^1 Y_{j,t}^{\frac{\nu-1}{\nu}}\,dj\right)^{\frac{\nu}{\nu-1} \cdot \frac{1}{\nu}} = Y_t^{1/\nu}$:
%
\begin{equation*}
    p_t\, Y_t^{1/\nu}\, Y_{j,t}^{-1/\nu} = p_{j,t}
\end{equation*}
%
Solving for $Y_{j,t}$:
%
\begin{align*}
    \left(\frac{Y_{j,t}}{Y_t}\right)^{-1/\nu} &= \frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t} \\[3mm]
    \frac{Y_{j,t}}{Y_t} &= \left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}
\end{align*}
%
Profit maximisation gives demand for variety $j$:
\begin{equation}
  Y_{j,t} = Y_t\left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu} \tag{A.2.2}
\end{equation}
%

\subsubsection{Intermediate Good Production} \label{appsec:int_good}
\paragraph{Cost Minimisation}
Before final goods can be aggregated, intermediate good producers use labor and capital as production inputs. To minimise production costs, they choose optimal factor quantities in each given time period. Therefore, we assume that these firms take the wage $W_t$, the rental rate of capital $P_t^k$ as well as nominal factor prices as given. The underlying production function requires these firms produce at least as much of the intermediate good as it is demanded at a given price. The intra-temporal intermediate firm problem results in 

\begin{equation}
    \min_{L_{j,t}, \tilde{K}_{j,t}} W_{t}L_{j,t} + P_t^k\tilde{K}_{j,t} \qquad \text{s.t.} \quad \tilde{K}^\alpha_{j,t}(z_tL_{j,t})^{1-\alpha} \geq \left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}Y_t \tag{A.2.3}
\end{equation}
%
where $z_t$ denotes the level of productivity and $\tilde{K}_{j,t} \equiv u_tK_{j,t}$ expresses effective capital.
%
The Lagrangian is
%
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{L} = W_t L_{j,t} + P_t^k \tilde{K}_{j,t} - \Lambda_{j,t}^F\left[\tilde{K}_{j,t}^\alpha(z_t L_{j,t})^{1-\alpha} - \left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}Y_t\right]
\end{equation*}
%
The first-order conditions with respect to $L_{j,t}$ and $\tilde{K}_{j,t}$ are:
%
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial L_{j,t}} = 0: \qquad & W_t = \Lambda_{j,t}^F(1-\alpha)\,\tilde{K}_{j,t}^\alpha\, z_t^{1-\alpha}\, L_{j,t}^{-\alpha} \\[6pt]
    \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \tilde{K}_{j,t}} = 0: \qquad & P_t^k = \Lambda_{j,t}^F\,\alpha\,\tilde{K}_{j,t}^{\alpha-1}(z_t L_{j,t})^{1-\alpha}
\end{align*}
%
Dividing the first condition by the second eliminates $\Lambda_{j,t}^F$:
%
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{W_t}{P_t^k} = \frac{(1-\alpha)\,\tilde{K}_{j,t}^\alpha\, z_t^{1-\alpha}\, L_{j,t}^{-\alpha}}{\alpha\,\tilde{K}_{j,t}^{\alpha-1}(z_t L_{j,t})^{1-\alpha}} = \frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\tilde{K}_{j,t}}{L_{j,t}}
\end{equation*}
%
which gives the optimal capital-labour ratio:
%
\begin{equation*}
    \tilde{K}_{j,t}^* = \frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\cdot\frac{W_t}{P_t^k}\cdot L_{j,t}
\end{equation*}
%
Substituting into the binding constraint $\tilde{K}_{j,t}^\alpha(z_t L_{j,t})^{1-\alpha} = \left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}Y_t$ yields factor demands. Since all intermediate firms face the same factor prices, they share a common capital--labour ratio, $\tilde K_{j,t}/L_{j,t}=\tilde K_t/L_t$ for every $j$, where $L_t=\int_0^1L_{j,t}\,dj$ and $\tilde K_t=\int_0^1\tilde K_{j,t}\,dj$ are aggregate labour and aggregate effective capital. Aggregate output is not yet $\tilde K_t^\alpha(z_tL_t)^{1-\alpha}$ as relative-price dispersion drives a wedge between the two, which is derived in \eqref{eq:agg_production} below. The aggregate real factor price conditions then follow from dividing the nominal first-order conditions by $p_t$ and using the common capital-labour ratio $\tilde K_{j,t}/L_{j,t}=\tilde K_t/L_t$, which makes $\tilde K_{j,t}^{\alpha}L_{j,t}^{-\alpha}=(\tilde K_t/L_t)^{\alpha}$ independent
of $j$. Hence
%
\begin{equation*}
    W_t = \Lambda_{j,t}(1-\alpha)
    \left(\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\cdot\frac{W_t}{P_t^k}\right)^\alpha
    z_t^{1-\alpha}\
\end{equation*}
%
where $L_{j,t}^\alpha$ cancels. Solving for $\Lambda_{j,t}$:
%
\begin{equation*}
    \Lambda_{j,t} = \frac{W_t}{(1-\alpha)\,z_t^{1-\alpha}}
    \left(\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{P_t^k}{W_t}\right)^\alpha
    = \frac{1}{z_t^{1-\alpha}}
    \left(\frac{W_t}{1-\alpha}\right)^{1-\alpha}
    \left(\frac{P_t^k}{\alpha}\right)^\alpha
\end{equation*}
%
Since $z_t^{1-\alpha}$ in the denominator can be absorbed into the wage term, this gives the nominal marginal cost $\Lambda_t^F$ , which is independent of $j$ under constant returns to scale:
%
\begin{equation*}
    \Lambda_t^F = \left(\frac{P_t^k}{\alpha}\right)^\alpha
    \left(\frac{W_t/z_t}{1-\alpha}\right)^{1-\alpha}
\end{equation*}
%
Dividing by the aggregate price level $p_t$ gives real marginal cost:
%
\begin{equation*}
    mc_t \equiv \frac{\Lambda_t^F}{p_t} =
    \left(\frac{r_t^k}{\alpha}\right)^\alpha
    \left(\frac{w_t/z_t}{1-\alpha}\right)^{1-\alpha}
\end{equation*}
%
where $r_t^k \equiv P_t^k/p_t$ and $w_t \equiv W_t/p_t$ denote the real rental rate and real wage respectively. The aggregate real factor price conditions then follow from dividing the nominal first-order conditions by $p_t$ and invoking $\tilde{K}_t^\alpha(z_tL_t)^{1-\alpha} = Y_t$. Hence, this yields aggregate real term FOC's
%
\begin{equation*}
    w_t = mc^*_t(1-\alpha)z_t^{1-\alpha}\tilde{K}_t^\alpha L_t^{-\alpha} \tag{A.2.4}
\end{equation*}
%
and
\begin{equation*}
    r_t^k = mc^*_t\cdot\alpha\cdot z_t^{1-\alpha}\tilde{K}_t^{\alpha-1} L_t^{1-\alpha}   \tag{A.2.5}
\end{equation*}
%
with
\begin{equation*}
    \tilde{K}_t \equiv u_t K_t \tag{A.2.6}
\end{equation*}
%
As we further follow \textcite{Gourio2012} for the disaster process, we assume that productivity grows at 

\begin{equation}
    \Gamma_t \equiv \frac{z_{t+1}}{z_t} = e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)+\mu+\epsilon_{z,t+1}} \tag{A.2.7}
\end{equation}
% 
\paragraph{Profit Maximisation}\label{appsec:profitmax}
We further assume Calvo price-setting, in which each intermediate good producer may reset its price with probability $1-\zeta$ in any given period, while with  probability $\zeta$ it keeps its price unchanged. A firm that resets its price  $p_{j,t}$ at time $t$ chooses it to maximise the present discounted value of  profits over all future periods in which the price may remain fixed, subject to the demand schedule derived in \eqref{eq:Cemand} holds in every future period 
$t+s$ in which $p_{j,t}$ remains fixed:
%
\begin{equation}
    \max_{p_{j,t}}\; \mathbb{E}_t \sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s\, Q_{t, t+s}\,
    \frac{p_{j,t}Y_{j,t+s} - W_{t+s}L_{j,t+s} - P^k_{t+s}\tilde{K}_{j,t+s}}{p_{t+s}}
    \tag{2.3.7}
\end{equation}
%
where $Q_{t,t+s}\equiv\prod_{i=1}^{s}Q_{t+i-1,t+i}$ is the household's \emph{real} stochastic discount factor inherited from \eqref{eq:SDF}, with $Q_{t,t}=1$. Since $Q_{t,t+s}$ prices real payoffs, nominal profits are deflated by $p_{t+s}$.
%
\begin{equation*}
    Y_{j,t+s} = \left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_{t+s}}\right)^{-\nu} Y_{t+s}
\end{equation*}
%
Nominal profits in period $t+s$ can be written as revenues minus total factor costs. Since total factor costs equal nominal marginal cost times output, i.e.\ $W_{t+s}L_{j,t+s} + P^k_{t+s}\tilde{K}_{j,t+s} = \Lambda_{t+s}^F Y_{j,t+s}$, substituting the demand schedule and deflating by $p_{t+s}$ gives real profits
%
\begin{align*}
  \frac{\bigl(p_{j,t}-\Lambda^F_{t+s}\bigr)Y_{j,t+s}}{p_{t+s}}
  &=\frac{1}{p_{t+s}}\left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_{t+s}}\right)^{-\nu}Y_{t+s}
     \bigl(p_{j,t}-\Lambda^F_{t+s}\bigr)\\[2mm]
  &=p_{t+s}^{\nu-1}\,Y_{t+s}\left(p_{j,t}^{1-\nu}-\Lambda^F_{t+s}\,p_{j,t}^{-\nu}\right)
\end{align*}
%
so that the objective becomes
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calvo_intertemporal}
    \max_{p_{j,t}}\; \mathbb{E}_t \sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s\, Q_{t, t+s}\,
    p_{t+s}^{\nu-1} Y_{t+s}
    \left(p_{j,t}^{1-\nu} - \Lambda^F_{t+s}\, p_{j,t}^{-\nu}\right)
    \tag{A.2.8b}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{First-order condition.}
Taking the derivative of \eqref{eq:calvo_intertemporal} with respect to 
$p_{j,t}$ and setting equal to zero:
%
\begin{equation*}
    E_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty (\zeta)^s Q_{t, t+s}\, p_{t+s}^{\nu-1} Y_{t+s}
    \left((1-\nu)\,p_{j,t}^{-\nu} + \nu\,\Lambda_{t+s}^F\, p_{j,t}^{-\nu-1}
    \right) = 0
\end{equation*}
%
Multiplying through by $p_{j,t}^{\nu+1}$ and noting $p_{j,t}$ does not depend 
on $s$:
%
\begin{equation*}
    E_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty (\zeta)^s Q_{t, t+s}\, p_{t+s}^{\nu-1} Y_{t+s}
    \left((1-\nu)\,p_{j,t} + \nu\,\Lambda_{t+s}^F\right) = 0
\end{equation*}
%
Solving for $p_{j,t}$ and taking expectations term by term:
%
\begin{equation*}
    p_{j,t}^* = \frac{\nu}{\nu-1}\,
    \frac{\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t, t+s}\,
          p_{t+s}^{\nu-1} Y_{t+s}\,\Lambda^F_{t+s}}
         {\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t, t+s}\,
          p_{t+s}^{\nu-1} Y_{t+s}}
    = \frac{\nu}{\nu-1}\,
    \frac{\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t, t+s}\,
          p_{t+s}^{\nu} Y_{t+s}\,mc^*_{t+s}}
         {\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t, t+s}\,
          p_{t+s}^{\nu-1} Y_{t+s}}
\end{equation*}
%
using $mc^*_{t+s}\equiv\Lambda^F_{t+s}/p_{t+s}$. Dividing the numerator by $p_t^{\nu}$ and the denominator by $p_t^{\nu-1}$ scales the ratio by $p_t^{-1}$; since all resetting firms choose the same price, $p_t^*=p_{j,t}^*$, and the left-hand side becomes
%
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{p_t^*}{p_t} = \frac{\nu}{\nu-1}\,
    \frac{\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t, t+s}
    \!\left(\frac{p_{t+s}}{p_t}\right)^{\!\nu} Y_{t+s}\,mc_{t+s}^*}
    {\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t, t+s}
    \!\left(\frac{p_{t+s}}{p_t}\right)^{\!\nu-1} Y_{t+s}}
\end{equation*}

Defining the gross reset inflation rate as $1+\pi_t^* \equiv p_t^*/p_{t-1}$  and the gross inflation rate as $1+\pi_t \equiv p_t/p_{t-1}$, such that $p_t^*/p_t = (1+\pi_t^*)/(1+\pi_t)$, the ratio above can be written recursively by introducing auxiliary variables $\Xi_{1_t}$ and $\Xi_{2_t}$ as in \textcite{Isore2017}
%
\begin{align*}
  \Xi_{1,t}&\equiv\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t,t+s}
    \!\left(\frac{p_{t+s}}{p_t}\right)^{\!\nu}Y_{t+s}\,mc^*_{t+s}, &
  \Xi_{2,t}&\equiv\mathbb{E}_t\sum_{s=0}^\infty \zeta^s Q_{t,t+s}
    \!\left(\frac{p_{t+s}}{p_t}\right)^{\!\nu-1}Y_{t+s}
\end{align*}
%
and splitting off the $s=0$ term, the factorisation $M_{t,t+s}=\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}M_{t+1,t+s}$ together with $(p_{t+s}/p_t)^{\nu}=(p_{t+1}/p_t)^{\nu}(p_{t+s}/p_{t+1})^{\nu}$ delivers the recursions
%
\begin{equation}\tag{A.2.9}
    1 + \pi_t^* = \frac{\nu}{\nu-1}\frac{\Xi_{1_t}}{\Xi_{2_t}}(1+\pi_t)
\end{equation}
%
where the recursive auxiliary variables are then defined as
%
\begin{align}
    \Xi_{1_t} &= Y_t mc_t^* + \zeta\, E_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}
    (1+\pi_{t+1})^\nu \,\Xi_{1_{t+1}}\right]\tag{A.2.10}\\[4pt]
    \Xi_{2_t} &= Y_t + \zeta\, E_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}
    (1+\pi_{t+1})^{\nu-1}\,\Xi_{2_{t+1}}\right]\tag{A.2.11}
\end{align}
%
with $mc_t^*$ the real marginal cost of production at time $t$.

\paragraph{Aggregated Inflation and Production}\label{appsec:agginflation}
In each period a fraction $1-\zeta$ of firms resets to the common price $p_t^*$ while the remaining fraction $\zeta$ carries last period's price. Because the non-adjusting firms are drawn at random, their price distribution coincides with the period-$(t-1)$ cross-sectional distribution, so for any exponent $k$, $\int_{1-\zeta}^{1}p_{j,t-1}^{k}\,dj=\zeta\int_0^1 p_{j,t-1}^{k}\,dj$. Applying this to the price index,
%
\begin{equation*}
  p_t^{1-\nu}
  =\int_0^{1-\zeta}\!\bigl(p_t^{*}\bigr)^{1-\nu}dj
   +\int_{1-\zeta}^{1}\! p_{j,t-1}^{1-\nu}\,dj
  =(1-\zeta)\bigl(p_t^{*}\bigr)^{1-\nu}+\zeta\,p_{t-1}^{1-\nu}
\end{equation*}
%
Dividing by $p_{t-1}^{1-\nu}$ and using $1+\pi_t\equiv p_t/p_{t-1}$ and
$1+\pi_t^{*}\equiv p_t^{*}/p_{t-1}$ gives the inflation rate
%
\begin{equation}
    (1+\pi_t)^{1-\nu} = (1-\zeta)(1+\pi_t^*)^{1-\nu} + \zeta \tag{A.2.12}
\end{equation}
%
With the Calvo assumption, the price-dispersion measure can likewise be written
recursively. Splitting the definition of $\Omega_t$ into resetting and
non-resetting firms and using the random-sampling property again,
%
\begin{align}\label{eq:omega}
  \Omega_t \;\equiv\; \int_0^1\!\left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}\!dj
  &= (1-\zeta)\left(\frac{p_t^{*}}{p_t}\right)^{\!-\nu}
     + p_t^{\nu}\!\int_{1-\zeta}^{1} p_{j,t-1}^{-\nu}\,dj \notag\\[2mm]
  &= (1-\zeta)\left(\frac{p_t^{*}}{p_t}\right)^{\!-\nu}
     + \zeta\left(\frac{p_t}{p_{t-1}}\right)^{\!\nu}
       \int_0^1\!\left(\frac{p_{j,t-1}}{p_{t-1}}\right)^{-\nu}\!dj \notag\\[2mm]
  &= (1-\zeta)\left(\frac{1+\pi_t}{1+\pi_t^{*}}\right)^{\!\nu}
     + \zeta\,(1+\pi_t)^{\nu}\,\Omega_{t-1}
     \tag{A.2.13}
\end{align}
%
where the last line uses $p_t/p_t^{*}=(1+\pi_t)/(1+\pi_t^{*})$ and
$p_t^{\nu}p_{t-1}^{-\nu}=(1+\pi_t)^{\nu}$.
%
This delivers the aggregate production function. Using the common capital--labour ratio, the binding cost-minimisation constraint
\eqref{eq:cost_minimisation_int} for firm $j$ can be written $(\tilde K_{j,t}/L_{j,t})^{\alpha}z_t^{1-\alpha}L_{j,t} =(p_{j,t}/p_t)^{-\nu}Y_t$. Integrating over $j\in[0,1]$ and pulling the common ratio out of the integral,
%
\begin{equation*}
    \left(\frac{\tilde{K}_t}{L_t}\right)^{\!\alpha} z_{t}^{1-\alpha}
    \int_0^1 L_{j,t}\,dj
    \;=\; Y_t\int_0^1\!\left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}\!dj
\end{equation*}
%
Labour-market clearing $\int_0^1 L_{j,t}\,dj = L_t$ then gives
%
\begin{equation*}
    \tilde{K}_t^\alpha(z_tL_t)^{1-\alpha}
    \;=\; Y_t\int_0^1\!\left(\frac{p_{j,t}}{p_t}\right)^{-\nu}\!dj
    \;=\; Y_t\,\Omega_t
\end{equation*}
%
so that
%
\begin{equation}
  Y_t=\frac{\tilde{K}_t^\alpha(z_tL_t)^{1-\alpha}}{\Omega_t} \tag{A.2.14}
\end{equation}
%
with $\Omega_t\geq 1$ the measure of relative price dispersion induced by sticky prices, equal to unity only when all firms charge $p_t$.
% ============================================================
%  APPENDIX A.3  —  BANKING BLOCK
%  Sovereign Disaster Risk, Banking Frictions and Macro Tail
%  Outcomes in a New Keynesian DSGE for the Euro Area
%
%  Structure:
%    A.3.1  Entrepreneurs (BGG 1999)
%    A.3.2  Banks (GK 2011 augmented with sovereign bonds)
%    A.3.3  Disaster Transmission Mechanism
%
%  Conventions:
%    - All equations in LEVEL (non-stationarized) form.
%      Stationarization is collected in Appendix B.
%    - Equation numbers continue from A.2 (first eq here is (A.X)).
%      Replace (A.X) counters with your running number before
%      submission; labels are set for \ref cross-referencing.
%    - Citations use biblatex author-year: \textcite / \parencite.
%    - Notation follows IS2017 exactly throughout.
%    - Departure boxes mark every deviation from IS2017.
%    - Stationarization flags note which detrending step applies.
% ============================================================

\subsection{Banking Block}\label{sec:banking_block}

This section derives the novel contribution of the paper. It combines the \textcite{Gertler2011} banking framework with \textcite{Bernanke1999} entrepreneur net-worth dynamics and a home-bias sovereign bond channel.  

% --- banker value function, incentive constraint, and leverage limit --- 
% Each equation is derived from first principles in level form following the three-step procedure of \textcite{Isore2017}.

% The two sub-blocks are linked through three rates: the deposit rate $R^d_t$ (determined in A.1 and passed up as $R^d_t = R^f_t$ via market clearing in Appendix~D), the loan rate $R^S_t$ (determined endogenously by the leverage constraint in A.3.2), and the return on capital $R^K_{t+1}$ (determined in A.3.1 and related to $R^S_t$ via the entrepreneur participation condition A.3.1.6, $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]=f(\cdot)R^S_t$).

% ============================================================
\subsubsection{Entrepreneurs}\label{subsec:entrepreneurs}

\paragraph{Setup and Contract Structure.}
The economy consists of a continuum of identical, risk-neutral entrepreneurs of mass one. Each purchase $K_{t+1}$ units of installed capital at the end of period~$t$ from the capital goods producer (Appendix~\ref{appsec:households}) at price $Q_t$ and rents capital to intermediate goods firms in period $t+1$ at the real rental rate $P^k_{t+1}$. capital that is retained at the same period is valued at the same price $Q_t$ that applies to newly purchased units. The framing of buying and selling capital used throughout this section is a standard accounting convention in this literature and not a literal transaction. A continuing entrepreneur need not physically dispose of capital it retains, since resale and purchase occur at the same price. Each entrepreneur has net worth $N^e_t$ and finances the gap $Q_tK_{t+1} - N^e_t$ by borrowing from a bank at gross real loan rate $R^S_t$, contracted at end of period~$t$.

An idiosyncratic productivity $\omega_{j,t+1}$ is drawn i.i.d.\ with $\mathbb{E}[\omega] = 1$ and c.d.f.\ $F(\cdot)$. The bank cannot observe $\omega_{j,t+1}$ without paying a monitoring cost to the entrepreneur's gross revenue, which is the costly state verification (CSV) problem of \textcite{Townsend1979}. The optimal contract specifies a default threshold $\bar{\omega}_t$ such that entrepreneur $j$ defaults if and only if $\omega_{j,t+1} < \bar{\omega}_t$. Because entrepreneurs are risk-neutral, they absorb aggregate risk and the participation constraint holds state-by-state after aggregate shocks to $R^K_{t+1}$ realise \parencite[Section~III]{Bernanke1999}. We assume the reduced-form external finance premiumof \textcite{Bernanke1999}, in which the contracting problem is summarised by the schedule \eqref{eq:A3_EFP}. Consequently neither $\bar\omega_t$ nor the monitoring parameter appears as a seperate object below, and the real resoures absorbed by monitoring are summarised in the premium rather than obtained seperately in the resource constraint \eqref{eq:goodclearing}.

$K_{t+1}$ here denotes the quantity committed to at the end of period $t$, before $x_{t+1}$ is realised while the capital law of motion's $K_{t+1}$ denotes the same object after the disaster realization, related by the Gourio factor.

\paragraph{Capital Accumulation and the Disaster Timing Convention.}
Let $K^n_{t+1}$ denote the quantity of installed capital contracted for at the
end of period $t$, before the disaster indicator $x_{t+1}$ is realised, and let
$K_{t+1}$ denote the stock actually available for production in period $t+1$,
after the realisation. The two are related by the \textcite{Gourio2012} factor,
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A3_Kgourio}
  K_{t+1} \;=\; e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)}\,K^n_{t+1}
  \tag{A.3.1.0a}
\end{equation}
%
so that capital is destroyed in the same proportion $(1-\Delta^k)$ as
productivity in \eqref{eq:prod_process}. The contracted quantity accumulates
from the surviving stock and the capital-goods producer's output,
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A3_LOM}
  K^n_{t+1} \;=\; \bigl(1-\delta_0 u_t^{\eta}\bigr)K_t
    \;+\; \mathcal{S}\!\left(\frac{I_t}{K_t}\right)K_t
  \tag{A.3.1.0b}
\end{equation}
%
with $\mathcal{S}(\cdot)$ the adjustment-cost technology of
paragraph~\ref{par:CP}. Throughout the entrepreneur's problem $K^n_{t+1}$ is the
object purchased and financed; $K_{t+1}$ is the object rented to intermediate
firms and entering $\tilde K_{t+1}=u_{t+1}K_{t+1}$. Every occurrence of the
capital stock below is one or the other and never both.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Utilisation Choice.}
\label{par:A311a}
 
Given capital $K_{t+1}$ acquired at $t$, the entrepreneur chooses period-$(t{+}1)$ utilisation $u_{t+1}$ to maximise gross revenue from the capital stock. Doing so takes as given the rental price $P^{k,real}_{t+1}$ as derived in the intermediate good producers cost minimisation resulting in equation~\ref{eq:resale_price} and the resale price $Q_{t+1}$ set by the capital-goods producer (Equation~\ref{eq:CP_TobinsQ}). Since the disaster factor $e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)}$ and the capital stock $K_{t+1}$ scale total revenue multiplicatively and do not depend on $u_{t+1}$, maximising realised revenue (for any realisation of $x_{t+1}$ and any idiosyncratic draw $\omega_{j,t+1}$) is equivalent to maximising per-unit revenue:
%
\begin{equation*}
  \max_{u_{t+1}}\;\;   P_{t+1}^{k, real}\,u_{t+1} + Q_{t+1}\bigl(1-\delta_0 u_{t+1}^{\eta}\bigr)
\end{equation*}
%
This objective is well-defined independently of the debt contract's default threshold $\bar\omega_t$. Therefore, utilisation maximises the total surplus available for division between entrepreneur and bank, not the entrepreneur's own share of it, so the choice is unaffected by contract details. The first-order condition is derived as 
%
\begin{equation}
  P_{t+1}^{k, real}  = Q_{t+1}\,\delta_0\,\eta\,u_{t+1}^{\eta-1}
  \tag{A.3.1.1}
\end{equation}
%
with second-order condition $-Q_{t+1}\delta_0\eta(\eta-1)u_{t+1}^{\eta-2}<0$, showing that the standard convexity assumption on the depreciation function is satisfied for $\eta>1$ which has been already imposed throughout the model. 

\paragraph{Consistency with the Capital-Goods Producer.}
The two margins that \textcite{Isore2017}'s household jointly optimises are now split across two agents, but both continue to price the same
market-clearing capital good. The capital-goods producer's investment condition~\eqref{eq:CP_TobinsQ}, initiated at date $t+1$, and the
entrepreneur's utilisation condition~\eqref{eq:A3_util} both determine the single date-$(t{+}1)$ price $Q_{t+1}$:
%
\begin{equation*}
  Q_{t+1}
  \;=\;\frac{1}{1-\tau\!\left(\dfrac{I_{t+1}}{K_{t+1}}-\dfrac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)}
  \;=\;\frac{r^k_{t+1}}{\delta_0\,\eta\,u_{t+1}^{\eta-1}}.
\end{equation*}
%
Eliminating $Q_{t+1}$ between the two recovers exactly \textcite{Isore2017}'s single combined household condition:
%
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{1}{1-\tau\!\left(\dfrac{I_{t+1}}{K_{t+1}}-\dfrac{\bar{I}}{\bar{K}}\right)} = \frac{r^k_{t+1}}{\delta_0\,\eta\; u_{t+1}^{\,\eta-1}}
\end{equation*}
%
confirming that the two-agent decomposition is algebraically equivalent to the original joint FOC.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Return on Capital}

 
Evaluated at the optimal utilisation \eqref{eq:A3_util}, the gross real return to holding one unit of capital from $t$ to $t+1$ is
 
\begin{equation}
  R^K_{t+1}
  = e^{x_{t+1}\ln(1-\Delta^k)}\,
    \frac{P^{k, real}_{t+1}\, u_{t+1}
          + Q_{t+1}\bigl(1 - \delta_0\, u_{t+1}^{\eta}\bigr)}
         {Q_t} \tag{A.3.1.2}
\end{equation}
%
where $u_{t+1}$ solves \eqref{eq:A3_util} and $\delta_{t+1} = \delta_0\, u_{t+1}^{\eta}$ is the resulting utilisation-dependent depreciation rate.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $R^K_{t+1}$ is a ratio of detrended prices and is already scale-invariant. No further detrending required. $\to$ Appendix~B.


% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{External Finance Premium.}


The CSV contracting problem of \textcite{Bernanke1999} yields a premium
schedule $f(\cdot)$ that is decreasing in the entrepreneur's equity share, but
the model is closed there in log-linear form and no level functional form is
stated. \textcite{Gelain2010} likewise works with the log-linearised
relationship
%
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{s}_t \;=\; -\,\chi^e\bigl(\hat{n}^e_t - \hat{q}_t - \hat{k}^n_{t+1}\bigr),
  \qquad
  \hat{s}_t \equiv \mathbb{E}_t\hat{r}^k_{t+1}-\hat{r}_t,
\end{equation*}
%
in which $\chi^e$ is the elasticity of the external finance premium with respect
to the leverage ratio and is the object estimated. Because the model here is
solved by third-order perturbation, a log-linear elasticity is not sufficient:
the second and third derivatives of $f(\cdot)$ enter the solution, so a level
form must be specified. We adopt the isoelastic schedule
%
\begin{equation}
  f\!\left(\frac{N^e_t}{Q_t K^n_{t+1}}\right)
  \;\equiv\; f_0
    \left(\frac{N^e_t}{Q_t K^n_{t+1}}\right)^{\!-\chi^e},
  \qquad \chi^e > 0,\; f_0 > 0\tag{A.3.1.3}
\end{equation}
%
which is the unique level form with constant elasticity $\chi^e$, and therefore
the unique form whose log-linearisation reproduces the relationship above
exactly at every point rather than only at the calibration point. It inherits
the sign properties of the \textcite{Bernanke1999} schedule: $f'<0$, so a fall
in $N^e_t$ widens the premium and amplifies the initial shock through the
financial accelerator. The choice is nonetheless an extrapolation of a locally
estimated elasticity to a global functional form, and the higher-order terms it
implies are not separately identified by the estimate; this is recorded as a
limitation in Section~\ref{sec:limitation}.

The parameter $f_0$ is a normalisation, not an independent structural
parameter: it is back-solved so that the schedule reproduces a target
steady-state entrepreneur premium $\mathbb{E}[\bar R^K]/\bar R^S$ at the target
equity ratio $\varkappa \equiv \bar N^e/(\bar Q\bar K^{n})$, giving
$f_0=\mathcal{F}\,\varkappa^{\chi^e}$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:D7}). The bank margin
$\bar R^S/\bar R^d$ is a separate calibration target, matched through the
leverage multiplier $\lambda$ rather than through $f_0$.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $N^e_t / Q_t K_{t+1}$ is a ratio of real quantities both growing at trend rate $Z_t$; the ratio is stationary after detrending by $Z_t$. Full stationarized form in Appendix~B. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Entrepreneur Net Worth Accumulation.}


Following further \textcite{Bernanke1999}, a fraction $\sigma^e \in (0,1)$ of entrepreneurs survive each period and carry their net worth forward. The exiting fraction $(1-\sigma^e)$ transfers net worth to households as a lump sum. A startup transfer $\iota_t^e$ from households to entering entrepreneurs prevents the entrepreneurs net worth to tend towards a value of zero:

\begin{equation}
  N^e_t
  = \sigma^e \!\left[
      \underbrace{R^K_t \cdot Q_{t-1}K^n_t}_{\text{gross return on capital}}
      - \underbrace{R^S_{t-1} \cdot\bigl(Q_{t-1}K^n_t - N^e_{t-1}\bigr)}_{\text{gross repayment of principal and interest}}
    \right]
    + \iota^e_t \tag{A.3.1.4}
\end{equation}
%
where $\iota^e_t \equiv \iota^e z_t$ is the startup transfer to entering
entrepreneurs, scaled by the productivity trend so that the detrended transfer
$\hat\iota^e = \iota^e$ is constant along the balanced growth path. A transfer
fixed in levels would vanish relative to net worth as $z_t$ grows and would not
support a steady state with $\bar{\hat n}^e>0$. The bank startup transfer
$\iota_t^b \equiv \iota z_t$ in \eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} is scaled identically.

In the standard \textcite{Bernanke1999} model without a banking sector, the lender's opportunity cost is the risk-free rate, so equation~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} places the premium between $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]$ and $R^f_t$ directly. Here the lender is a constrained bank whose required return on the loan book is $R^S_t>R^d_t$, determined by the binding leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323} together with loan-market clearing (A.5.4).  The premium of~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} then applies on top of $R^S_t$ rather than on top of $R^f_t$, which is the sense in which the two frictions stack.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $R^K_t \cdot Q_{t-1}K_t$ has dimension $Z_{t-1}$. Divide through by $Z_{t-1}$ and apply the Gourio-trick growth normalization. Full stationarized form: $\hat{n}^e_t = \sigma^e e^{-\mu}[\cdots] + \hat{\iota}^e$. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Entrepreneur Balance Sheet Identity}


The value to entrepreneurs equals the funding gap:

\begin{equation}\label{appeq:A3_EB}
  Q_tS_t \;\equiv\; Q_t K_{t+1}^n - N^e_t \tag{A.3.1.5}
\end{equation}
%
which constitutes an accounting identity, defining the quantity $Q_tS_t$ that enters the bank balance sheet equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bankBS} and the leverage constraint equation~\eqref{eq:A323}.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Divide through by $Z_t$. All three terms detrend directly. $\to$ Appendix~B.


% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Capital Demand under the Optimal Contract}


Financing of $K^n_{t+1}$ is decided at the end of period $t$, taking $Q_t$ (capital-goods producer, Appendix~\ref{appsec:households}), the loan rate $R^S_t$, and the premium schedule~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} as given. An individual entrepreneur is atomistic relative to the continuum and therefore treats both $R^S_t$ and the aggregate equity ratio entering $f(\cdot)$ parametrically: it does not perceive its own $K^n_{t+1}$ choice as moving either.

Were external finance frictionless, expected end-of-period profit would be
%
\begin{equation*}
  \mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]\,Q_tK^n_{t+1} - R^S_t\bigl(Q_tK^n_{t+1}-N^e_t\bigr)
  \;=\;
  \bigl(\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]-R^S_t\bigr)Q_tK^n_{t+1} + R^S_tN^e_t,
\end{equation*}
%
which is linear in $K^n_{t+1}$. A determinate, finite, positive level of capital demand is then an equilibrium only if the coefficient on $K^n_{t+1}$ vanishes: at any strictly positive coefficient the atomistic entrepreneur prefers $K^n_{t+1}\to\infty$, and at any strictly negative coefficient it prefers $K^n_{t+1}=0$. This free-entry benchmark gives $\mathbb{E}_t[R^K_{t+1}]=R^S_t$.

Under costly state verification the benchmark does not survive, because the expected unit cost of external funds exceeds $R^S_t$: the lender must be compensated in expectation for monitoring and default losses, and those losses rise with the amount borrowed per unit of the entrepreneur's own net worth. The optimal contract of \textcite{Bernanke1999} maximises the entrepreneur's expected share of the project return subject to the lender breaking even at $R^S_t$; its first-order condition in $K^n_{t+1}$ replaces the free-entry condition by
%
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[R^K_{t+1}\right]
  = f\!\left(\frac{N_t^e}{Q_tK^n_{t+1}}\right) R^S_t \tag{A.3.1.6}
\end{equation}
%
with $f(\cdot)\geq 1$ and $f'(\cdot)<0$ as parameterised in \eqref{eq:A3_EFP}. The free-entry benchmark is nested at $f\equiv 1$, which obtains when monitoring is costless; the wedge $f(\cdot)-1$ is the external finance premium. Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} is a condition that $R^S_t$ must satisfy; it does not by itself determine $R^S_t$.  The equilibrium loan rate is pinned by the balance sheet chain rather than by ~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} itself. Given $N^b_t$ from~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} the binding leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323} and the home-bias identity~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias} fix loan supply $Q_tS_t=(1-\phi)\lambda N^b_t$; the entrepreneur balance sheet~\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS} then fixes $Q_tK_{t+1}=Q_tS_t+N^e_t$ and hence the equity ratio entering $f(\cdot)$. Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_indiff} returns the $R^S_t$ consistent with that quantity. Loan-market clearing~\eqref{eq:loanmarketclearing} is the statement that these two routes to $Q_tS_t$ agree; it is a consequence of the optimality conditions, not an addition to them. This is the channel through which a sovereign loss on bank balance sheets raises the cost of credit to entrepreneurs.

The mechanics of that channel on bank leverage determination and how it responds to sovereign risk, are developed next.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Both sides are gross rates --- scale-invariant. $\to$ Appendix~B.
 
% \medskip
% \noindent\textit{Dynare implementation:} \texttt{RL = RK(+1)}.


\subsubsection{Banks with Sovereign Bonds}
\label{appsec:banksov}

A continuum of identical banks of mass one each raise deposits $D_{j,t}$ from households at gross real deposit rate $R^d_t$, accumulate net worth
$N^b_{j,t}$ from retained earnings, and hold two asset classes: loans $L_{j,t}$ to entrepreneurs at gross real loan rate $R^S_t$, and domestic sovereign bonds $B^b_{j,t}$ earning gross real return $R^b_t$. The portfolio share of sovereign bonds in total bank assets is fixed by the home-bias parameter $\phi \in [0,1)$.

Banks are owned by households. To motivate an endogenous constraint on bank leverage, \textcite{Gertler2011} introduce a moral hazard friction: at the start of each period, a banker can divert a fraction $\theta^b$ of managed assets and transfer them to the owning household as private gain. Depositors lend only if the continuation value of operating exceeds the gain from diverting; this incentive compatibility condition becomes the leverage constraint in equation~\eqref{eq:A323}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Bank Balance Sheet Identity}


\begin{equation}
  \underbrace{\,Q_t S_{j,t}}_{\text{loans}}
  + \underbrace{\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}}_{\text{sovereign bonds}}
  =
  \underbrace{N^b_{j,t}}_{\text{net worth}}
  + \underbrace{D_{j,t}}_{\text{deposits}} \tag{A.3.2.1}
\end{equation}
%
The home-bias parameter $\phi$ splits bank assets between loans and sovereign bonds. When $\phi = 0$ the model collapses to the standard \textcite{Gertler2011} specification with loans only. Bankers then maximise continuation value subject to a moral-hazard incentive constraint. Both value function and the constraint are linear in net worth and assets, which is established in paragraph ~\ref{par:A322} and is what reduces this constraint to a leverage gap.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\paragraph{Banker Value Function and Optimisation}\label{par:A322}
 
A bank $j$ carries net worth $n_{j,t}$ in the end of period $t$ and solves a recursive programme to maximise the expected discounted value of lifetime payoffs to the owning household. With probability $\sigma^b\in(0,1)$ the banker survives to period $t+1$ and continues intermediating; with probability $1-\sigma^b$ it exits and returns $n_{j,t+1}$ directly to the household. All discounting is at the household stochastic discount factor $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$, since bankers are a share of the households. The Bellman equation is
 
\begin{equation*}
    V^b_t(n_{j,t})
    \;=\;
    \max_{\{S_{j,t},\,B^b_{j,t},\,D_{j,t}\}}\;
    \mathrm{E}_t\!\left[
        \mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}
        \Bigl\{
            (1-\sigma^b)\,n_{j,t+1}
            +\sigma^b\,V^b_{t+1}(n_{j,t+1})
        \Bigr\}
    \right]
\end{equation*}
 
\noindent subject to the bank balance sheet identity \eqref{eq:A3_bankBS} and the incentive-compatibility condition derived below. 

Therefore, let $A_{j,t} \equiv Q_t S_{j,t}+\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}$ denote effective bank assets as defined in \eqref{eq:A3_bankBS}. The balance sheet identity gives $D_{j,t}=A_{j,t}-n_{j,t}$, so the deposit liability can be eliminated. Computing returns on each asset class and factoring out the deposit cost:
 
\begin{align*}
n_{j,t+1}
&= R^S_t\,Q_t S_{j,t}
 + R^b_{t+1}\,\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}
 - R^d_t\,D_{j,t}\\[4pt]
&= \underbrace{(R^S_t-R^d_t)\,Q_t S_{j,t}}_{\text{excess return on loans}}
 + \underbrace{(R^b_{t+1}-R^d_t)\,\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}}_{\text{excess return on bonds}}
 + R^d_t\,n_{j,t}.
\end{align*}
 
As the portfolio composition is fixed at the common structural share $\phi$ (paragraph~\ref{par:A324}), $Q_tS_{j,t}=(1-\phi)A_{j,t}$ and $Q^b_tB^b_{j,t}=\phi A_{j,t}$ for every $j$, so the effective-asset-weighted excess return
%
\begin{equation*}
    \bar{R}^{\,\mathrm{ex}}_{t+1}
    \;\equiv\;
    \frac{(R^S_t-R^d_t)Q_t S_{j,t}
         +(R^b_{t+1}-R^d_t)\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}}{A_{j,t}}
    \;=\;
    (1-\phi)\bigl(R^S_t-R^d_t\bigr)+\phi\bigl(R^b_{t+1}-R^d_t\bigr)
\end{equation*}
%
is a function of aggregate prices and the structural share alone. It is
therefore common across banks and independent of bank~$j$'s balance-sheet
scale, which is what makes the value function linear below. We now want to prove that the period-$(t+1)$ value function is linear in both net worth and effective assets,
%
\begin{equation*}
    V^b_{t+1}(n_{j,t+1})
    \;=\;
    \nu_{t+1}\,n_{j,t+1} + \eta_{t+1}\,A_{j,t+1},
\end{equation*}
with a marginal value of a unit coefficients of net worth $\nu_{t+1}$ and effective assets $\eta_{t+1}$. Substituting into the continuation payoff:
%
\begin{equation*}
    (1-\sigma^b)\,n_{j,t+1}+\sigma^b\,V^b_{t+1}(n_{j,t+1})
    \;=\;
    \bigl[(1-\sigma^b)+\sigma^b\,\nu_{t+1}\bigr]\,n_{j,t+1}
    \;+\;\sigma^b\,\eta_{t+1}\,A_{j,t+1}.
\end{equation*}
%
Since the leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323} binds in every period,
$A_{j,t+1}=\lambda_{t+1}\,n_{j,t+1}$, and substituting and collecting terms
yields
%
\begin{equation*}
    (1-\sigma^b)\,n_{j,t+1}+\sigma^b\,V^b_{t+1}(n_{j,t+1})
    \;=\;
    \underbrace{%
        \bigl[(1-\sigma^b)+\sigma^b\,(\nu^b_{t+1}+\lambda_{t+1}\,\eta^b_{t+1})\bigr]
    }_{\displaystyle\Omega^b_t}
    \;n_{j,t+1}
\end{equation*}
%
where $\Omega^b_t$ is the survival-weighted continuation value. Substituting the net-worth law of motion, the objective becomes linear in $A_{j,t}$ with slope $\mathbb{E}_t[Q_{t,t+1}\Omega^b_t\bar R^{\,\mathrm{ex}}_{t+1}]$. Whenever that slope is strictly positive the bank raises $A_{j,t}$ up to the boundary of the incentive constraint derived below, so the maximum is attained at the constraint and the value function is the objective read as a function of
$A_{j,t}$:
%
\begin{align*}
V^b_t(n_{j,t})
&= \mathrm{E}_t\!\left[
       Q_{t,t+1}\,\Omega^b_t
       \bigl(\bar{R}^{\,\mathrm{ex}}_{t+1}\,A_{j,t}+R^d_t\,n_{j,t}\bigr)
   \right]\\[6pt]
&= \underbrace{\mathrm{E}_t\!\left[Q_{t,t+1}\,\Omega^b_t\,R^d_t\right]}%
     _{\displaystyle \nu^b_t}\!\cdot n_{j,t}
   \;+\;
   \underbrace{\mathrm{E}_t\!\left[Q_{t,t+1}\,\Omega^b_t\,
     \bar{R}^{\,\mathrm{ex}}_{t+1}\right]}_{\displaystyle \eta^b_t}\!\cdot A_{j,t}
\end{align*}
%
Both coefficients depend only on aggregate prices, the structural share $\phi$
and the stochastic discount factor, not on bank~$j$'s portfolio scale. The
linear conjecture is therefore verified, with
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Banker_Value}\tag{A.3.2.2}
    V^b_t(n_{j,t}) \;=\; \nu^b_t\,n_{j,t} \;+\; \eta^b_t\,A_{j,t}
\end{equation}
%
Linearity holds because the bank is a price-taker in both asset markets, meaning the excess returns $R^S_{t+1}-R^d_t$ and $R^b_{t+1}-R^d_t$ are determined by aggregate conditions and are independent of bank~$j$'s balance sheet size.
 
\paragraph{Incentive Constraint and Leverage Limit.}
 
The diversion condition requires that the continuation value of honest operation exceeds the private gain from diverting the fraction
$\theta^b$ of managed assets:
\begin{equation*}
    V^b_t(n_{j,t})
    \;\geq\;
    \theta^b\,
    \bigl[Q_t S_{j,t}+\,Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}\bigr]
    \;=\;
    \theta^b\,A_{j,t}.
\end{equation*}
Substituting the linear form~(\ref{eq:Banker_Value}):
\begin{equation*}
     \nu^b_t\,n_{j,t}+\eta^b_t\,A_{j,t}\;\geq\;\theta^b\,A_{j,t}
    \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
    \bigl(\theta^b-\eta^b_t\bigr)A_{j,t}\;\leq\;\nu^b_t\,n_{j,t}
\end{equation*}
Provided the diversion parameter this exceeds the marginal franchise value of assets and rearanges to 

\begin{equation*}
    A_{j,t}\;\leq\;\frac{\nu^b_t}{\theta^b-\eta^b_t}\,n_{j,t}
    \;\equiv\;\lambda_t\,n_{j,t}
\end{equation*}

Bankers expand assets to the limit whenever $\eta_t>0$ and therefore the franchise value of asset holdings is positive relative to the outside option of
depositing at $R^d_t$, so the constraint binds with equality in equilibrium. This gives the individual bank's leverage constraint
 
\begin{equation}\tag{A.3.2.3}
    \,Q_t S_{j,t}+Q^b_t B^b_{j,t}
    \;\leq\;
    \lambda_t\,N^b_{j,t}
\end{equation}
% 
and in aggregated terms, since it binds for every $j$ and is linear, integrating over $j\in[0,1]$ gives 
%
\begin{align*}
    \,Q_t S_{t}+Q^b_t B^b_{t}
    \;=\;
    \lambda_t\,N^b_{t} \qquad \text{where} \qquad S_t &= \int_0^1 S_{j,t} ~dj \\[4mm]
     N^b_t &= \int_0^1 N_{j,t} ~dj 
\end{align*}
 
\noindent \noindent with time-varying multiplier $\lambda_t=\nu^b_t/(\theta^b-\eta^b_t)$.
Both $\nu^b_t$ and $\eta^b_t$ are forward-looking and are carried as separate endogenous variables, so $\lambda_t$ is solved each period from their recursions rather than fixed at its steady-state value. The constraint is imposed as a binding equality throughout, following \textcite{Gertler2011}, who verify that it binds in a neighbourhood of the steady state under empirically reasonable parameter values. The steady-state values $\bar\nu^b$, $\bar\eta^b$ and hence $\bar\lambda$ are derived in Appendix~\ref{sec:SteadyState}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Home-Bias Portfolio Identity}


In the euro-area institutional context, domestic banks are the predominant holders of domestic sovereign debt, reflecting the zero risk-weight treatment of home-country government bonds under Basel capital requirements, their eligibility as high-quality liquid assets and the structural incentives that systematically favour domestic over cross-border sovereign bond holdings \parencite{Gennaioli2014,Bocola2016}

The parameter $\phi \in [0,1)$ captures this as a structural characteristic of the bank balance sheet rather than a portfolio decision variable. Given these institutional frictions banks are effectively price-takers in the sovereign bond market at the composition margin, and $\phi$ is calibrated to the observed average domestic sovereign bond share in euro-area bank total assets. Formally, $\phi$ is defined as the bond share of total assets $A_{j,t}\equiv Q_tS_{j,t}+Q^b_tB^b_{j,t}$ (as prior defined in paragraph \ref{par:A322}): 
%
\begin{equation*}
  Q^b_t B^b_{j,t} = \phi\, A_{j,t}, \qquad Q_t S_{j,t} = (1-\phi)\, A_{j,t}.
\end{equation*}
%
Dividing the two eliminates $A_{j,t}$ and gives the portfolio condition directly; since $\phi$ is common across banks, integrating over $j\in[0,1]$ leaves it unchanged in the aggregate:
%
\begin{equation}
  Q^b_t B^b_t \;=\; \frac{\phi}{1-\phi}\,Q_tS_t
  \tag{A.3.2.4}
\end{equation}
%
Combined with the entrepreneur balance sheet~\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS},
equation~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias} closes the bank asset side as $Q_tS_t$ is pinned by entrepreneur equity and the binding leverage constraint \eqref{eq:A323}, and $Q^b_t B^b_t$ is then determined proportionally. The counterfactual sets $\phi = 0$, which eliminates the sovereign bond portfolio to the standard \textcite{Gertler2011} specification with loans only, shutting down the sovereign-bank transmission channel while preserving the standard BGG financial accelerator.

\paragraph{Bank Net Worth Accumulation.}


Aggregating across surviving bankers $\sigma^b$ and adding
startup transfers $\iota_t^b = \iota^b z_t$ to newly entering bankers, the \textcite{Gertler2011} augmented bank net worth accumulation equation reads as

\begin{equation}
  N^b_t
  = \sigma^b
    \Bigl[
      (R^S_{t-1} - R^d_{t-1})\,Q_{t-1}S_{t-1}
      + (R^b_t - R^d_{t-1})\,\,Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}
      + R^d_{t-1}\,N^b_{t-1}
    \Bigr]
    + \iota_t^b \tag{A.3.2.5}
\end{equation}
%
Surviving bankers earn at first the excess return on loans $(R^S_{t-1} - R^d_{t-1})$ on their loan portfolio, secondly the excess return on sovereign bonds $(R^b_t - R^d_{t-1})$ on their bond portfolio and thirdly the deposit rate on their existing net worth.

% When $\theta_t$ rises, $Q^b_t$ falls (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}), which reduces the mark-to-market value $Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}$ and compresses $R^b_t$ (equation~\eqref{eq:A3_Rb}). The second term in brackets in~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} therefore falls, eroding $N^b_t$ without any realised defaults, purely through the anticipated sovereign haircut. This is the sovereign-bank channel.



% ============================================================
\subsubsection{Disaster Transmission Mechanism}
\label{sec:Disaster_Transition}

This subsection derives how $\theta_t$ propagates through the banking block. It establishes the bank's sovereign bond pricing condition from first principles before applying the \textcite{Gabaix2012} linearity-generating process to obtain a closed-form bond price. This paper sets $\Delta^k>0$ following \textcite{Isore2017}, so capital (and productivity) are destroyed proportionally in the disaster state and $\beta(\theta_t)$ is time-varying per equation~\eqref{eq:betatheta}. Since portfolio composition is fixed by the structural parameter $\phi$ the bank's single optimisation marginal is total asset scale $A_{j,t}$ which is set at $\lambda_t N^b_{j,t}$ by the binding leverage constraint \eqref{eq:A323}. The sovereign bond price $Q^b_t$ therefore clears the bond market at the fixed composition ratio rather than being determined by a separate portfolio first-order condition. The structural condition that defines the sovereign-bank channel is $\phi>0$ together with $\Delta^b>0$: banks hold domestic bonds and absorb disaster losses through their balance sheets. The counterfactual $\phi=0$ removes the sovereign-bank channel while preserving the \textcite{Isore2017} capital channel, allowing a clean decomposition of the two transmission mechanisms. All disaster transmission through bonds operates via equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Equilibrium Bond Pricing Condition}


With portfolio composition pinned by the structural parameter $\phi$, sovereign bonds are not a free choice variable for the individual bank: once total assets are fixed at $A_{j,t}=\lambda_t N^b_{j,t}$ by the binding leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323}, the bond position follows residually as
$Q^b_tB^b_{j,t}=\phi A_{j,t}$. No portfolio first-order condition in $B^b_{j,t}$ is therefore available, and $Q^b_t$ must be determined outside the
constrained bank's problem.

We therefore assume that the sovereign bond is priced by an unconstrained marginal investor who discounts at the household stochastic discount factor $\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}$ of~\eqref{eq:SDF}, so that
%
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\, R^b_{t+1}\right] = 1
  \tag{A.3.3.1}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent Two remarks make the role of this assumption precise. First, it is not implied by the bank's optimisation. A bank whose leverage constraint binds has $\eta^b_t>0$ and values an additional unit of any asset above its household-SDF price; imposing~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} amounts to assuming that the marginal holder of the debt is not the constrained domestic bank. This is the pricing-side counterpart of treating $\phi$ as a structural balance-sheet characteristic rather than a portfolio choice on the quantity side, and it is what allows the \textcite{Gabaix2012} closed form of the next paragraph to be used. Second, given $Q^b_t$ from~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} and the \emph{value} $Q^b_tB^b_t$ from the home-bias identity~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias}, it is the outstanding \emph{quantity} $B^b_t$ that is determined residually, with the government accommodating it and lump-sum taxes $T_t$ adjusting through~\eqref{eq:A4_govBC}. Bond supply is perfectly elastic at the price~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}; there is no separate debt rule, and the block is neither over- nor under-determined.

\paragraph{Asset-Specific Resilience.}

 
Following \textcite{Gabaix2012}, the linearity-generating process assigns each risky asset an asset-specific resilience that summarizes the disaster-state expected return loss. For the sovereign bond paying $(1 - \Delta^b)$ in the disaster state and $1$ in the BAU state we can state that
 
\begin{equation}
  H^b_t \;=\; 1 - \theta_t \cdot \Delta^b \cdot \tilde\Lambda^M_t
  \tag{A.3.3.2}
\end{equation}
%
where $\Delta^b \in (0,1)$ is the sovereign haircut in a disaster and
%
\begin{equation*}
  \tilde\Lambda^M_t
  \;\equiv\;
  \frac{\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}^d_{t,t+1}\right]}
       {\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}\right]}
\end{equation*}

%
is the disaster SDF loading, normalised by the conditional mean of the stochastic discount factor rather than by its no-disaster realisation.  The normalisation is what makes $H^b_t$ commensurate with the risk-free rate $R^f_t=1/\mathbb{E}_t[M_{t+1}]$ against which it is divided in~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}; without it the bond Euler~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} would hold only to first order in $\theta_t$.  $H^b_t$ captures the disaster-probability-weighted reduction in the bond's risk-adjusted payoff.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Sovereign Bond Price}
\label{par:A333}

Following \textcite{Gabaix2012} for the closed-form expression for $Q^b_t$ via the linearity-generating process and \textcite{Isore2017} for their risky-bond extension implementation, we substitute the gross real return $R^b_{t+1} = (1 - x_{t+1}\Delta^b)/Q^b_t$ into the bond Euler~\eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler} and by taking expectations:

\begin{equation*}
  \frac{1}{Q^b_t}\,
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}
  \bigl(1 - x_{t+1}\Delta^b\bigr)\right] = 1
\end{equation*}
%
We can then apply the \textcite{Gabaix2012} decomposition, separating the two states of $x_{t+1}$ in the expectation and using
$\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]=1/R^f_t$,
%
\begin{align*}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\bigl(1-x_{t+1}\Delta^b\bigr)\right]
  &= \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\right]
     -\Delta^b\,\theta_t\,M^{d}_{t+1} \\[3pt]
  &= \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}\right]
     \left(1-\Delta^b\,\theta_t\,
     \frac{M^{d}_{t+1}}{\mathbb{E}_t[\mathcal{M}_{t,t+1}]}\right)
   \tag{A.3.3.3}
\end{align*}
%
Substituting back into the bond Euler \eqref{eq:A3_bondeuler}, the closed-form sovereign bond price is
%
\begin{equation}
  Q^b_t \;=\; \frac{H^b_t}{R^f_t}
  \;=\;\frac{1-\theta_t\,\Delta^b\,\tilde\Lambda^M_t}{R^f_t}
  \tag{A.3.3.3b}
\end{equation}
%

%
% \parencite[Appendix~A.8, Eq.~A.40]{Isore2017};
% \parencite[Eq.~4]{Gabaix2012}.
% 

% \textcite{Isore2017} allow capital destruction ($\Delta^k > 0$),  which modifies $\beta(\theta_t)$ and introduces a disaster factor in the household capital Euler.
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $Q^b_t$ is a ratio of dimensionless objects; $R^f_t$ is a gross rate. Both are scale-invariant. $\to$ Appendix~B.
 
% \medskip
% \noindent\textit{Dynare implementation note:}
% Equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice} is used directly in Dynare as a closed-form expression in place of iterating on the full bond Euler. It is exact at the non-stochastic steady state and correct to first order in perturbation. At third order, small deviations from the full bond Euler may arise; these are accepted as the cost of preserving tractability, consistent with the approach of \textcite{Isore2017} in their risky-bond extension.


% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Sovereign Bond Gross Return}

 
The one-period gross real return on the sovereign bond, accounting
for the disaster haircut:
 
\begin{equation}
  R^b_{t+1}
  = \frac{1 - x_{t+1}\,\Delta^b}{Q^b_t}
  \tag{A.3.3.4}
\end{equation}
%
where $x_{t+1} \in \{0,1\}$ is the disaster realisation at the
start of period $t+1$, with $R^b_{t+1} = 1/Q^b_t$ in the BAU state and $R^b_{t+1} = (1-\Delta^b)/Q^b_t < 1/Q^b_t$ in the disaster state.
%  
Taking the conditional expectation of equation~\eqref{eq:A3_Rb} and substituting
equation~\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}, we arrive at the expected sovereign bond gross return:

\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{E}_t\!\left[R^b_{t+1}\right]
  = \frac{1 - \theta_t\,\Delta^b}{Q^b_t}
  = \frac{(1 - \theta_t\,\Delta^b)\,R^f_t}
         {1 - \theta_t\,\Delta^b\,\tilde\Lambda^M_t}
  \tag{A.3.3.5}
\end{equation}
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $R^b_{t+1}$ is a gross rate --- scale-invariant. $\to$ Appendix~B.
The sovereign spread is defined as the excess of the bond's promised gross yield over the risk-free rate,
%
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{spread}_t
  \;\equiv\; \frac{1}{Q^b_t} - R^f_t
  \;=\; R^f_t\,\frac{\theta_t\,\Delta^b\,\tilde\Lambda^M_t}{H^b_t}
  \;\approx\; \theta_t \cdot \Delta^b \cdot \tilde\Lambda^M_t
  \tag{A.3.3.6}
\end{equation}
%
The approximation holds for $R^f_t\approx1$ and small $\theta_t\Delta^b\tilde\Lambda^M_t$, which is appropriate given the calibration of $\theta_t$ to rare-event frequencies following \textcite{Barro2008}.

Disaster risk reaches real activity through two routes that must be kept distinct. The first is a pure preference-and-pricing effect: a rise in $\theta_t$ shifts the pseudo-discount factor $\beta(\theta_t)$ of~\eqref{eq:betatheta}, moves the risk-free rate, and raises the sovereign spread
of~\eqref{eq:A3_spread} on impact. This route requires no default ever to occur, but it does \emph{not} operate through bank balance sheets: net worth
accumulation~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} applies the realised return $R^b_t$ to the \emph{predetermined} position $Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}$, so a change in $\theta_t$
leaves $N^b_t$ unaffected at $t$. The second route is the sovereign--bank channel proper and requires a realised disaster, $x_t=1$: the haircut $\Delta^b$ lowers $R^b_t$, erodes $N^b_t$ through~\eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth}, tightens the leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323}, contracts loan supply, and raises the external finance premium of~\eqref{eq:A3_EFP} through the fall in the entrepreneur equity  ratio. Only the second route is amplified by $\phi$, and it is the one the $\phi=0$ counterfactual switches off. With one-period bonds no mark-to-market channel is available: bank asset values are not revalued by current-period news about $\theta_t$.


\subparagraph{Derivation of Closed Form $\Lambda^M$}

Under the Epstein-Zin preference given in~\eqref{app:SDF}, one can consider two date $(t, t{+}1)$
realisations that only differ in the disaster indicator $x_{t+1}$. Because \eqref{eq:prod_process} makes the disaster a permanent one-off
contraction in the level of productivity, and because capital contracts in the
same proportion by \eqref{eq:A3_Kgourio}, every trend-growing quantity from
$t+1$ onward is scaled by the same factor $(1-\Delta^k)$ in the disaster state.
The value function $\tilde V_t$ is homogeneous of degree one in these
quantities, so $C^{d}_{t+1}=(1-\Delta^k)C^{f}_{t+1}$ and
$\tilde V^{d}_{t+1}=(1-\Delta^k)\tilde V^{f}_{t+1}$, while hours are stationary
and unaffected, $L^d_{t+1}=L^f_{t+1}$. The certainty equivalent
$\mathcal{R}_t$ and the constant $\beta_0$ are measurable at $t$ and identical
across the two realisations, so both cancel from the ratio. The certainty equivalent $\mathcal{R}_t(\cdot)$ is measurable at $t$ and identical across the two time realisations, hence

\begin{align*}
\Lambda^M &= \left(\frac{C^d_{t+1}}{C^f_{t+1}}\right)^{-\psi}\left(\frac{\tilde{V}^d_{t+1}}{\tilde{V}^f_{t+1}}\right)^{\psi-\gamma} \\[4mm] &= (1-\Delta^k)^{-\psi}(1-\Delta^k)^{\psi-\gamma} \\[4mm] &= (1 - \Delta^k)^{-\gamma} \qquad \text{(raw loading)}\tag{A.3.3.2b}\label{eq:A3_LambdaM}
\end{align*}
%
The object derived above is the ratio of the disaster-state SDF to its no-disaster realisation, $\Lambda^M \equiv\mathcal{M}^d_{t,t+1}/\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1} = (1-\Delta^k)^{-\gamma}$, and depends on the disaster size and risk aversion alone.  The normalisation required by~\eqref{eq:A3_resil} follows by taking the conditional mean of the same object.  Since $M_{t+1}=\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}$ with probability $1-\theta_t$ and $M_{t+1}=\Lambda^M \mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}$ with probability $\theta_t$,
%
\begin{equation}
  \frac{\mathbb{E}_t\!\left[M_{t+1}\right]}{\mathcal{M}^f_{t,t+1}}
  = (1-\theta_t)+\theta_t\Lambda^M
  = 1+\theta_t\bigl(\Lambda^M-1\bigr)
  \;\equiv\;\mathcal{E}(\theta_t),
  \qquad
  \tilde\Lambda^M_t=\frac{\Lambda^M}{\mathcal{E}(\theta_t)}
  \tag{A.3.3.2c}
  \label{eq:A3_LambdaMtilde}
\end{equation}
%
$\mathcal{E}(\theta_t)$ is the same moment that governs the risk-free rate in equation~\eqref{eq:B_EQ}; the two appearances are not a coincidence but the same normalisation of the stochastic discount factor, entering once through the denominator of $R^f_t$ and once through the resilience of the defaultable bond.  Unlike $\Lambda^M$, $\tilde\Lambda^M_t$ is mildly time-varying through $\theta_t$; it is decreasing in $\theta_t$, so that a rise in disaster probability raises the spread less than proportionally.


% ============================================================
%  END OF APPENDIX A.3
% ============================================================

% ============================================================
%  BEGIN OF APPENDIX A.4
% ============================================================

% ============================================================
\subsection{Public Authority}
\label{sec:public}
 
Following \textcite{Isore2017}, the public authority issues one-period sovereign bonds, collects lump-sum taxes from households, and sets the nominal interest rate via a Taylor-type rule. No government expenditure on goods enters the model; fiscal policy is reduced to the financing of outstanding debt through taxes and new bond issuance.
 
% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Government Budget Constraint}
\label{par:A411}

The government issues one-period sovereign bonds at the end of period $t$: a quantity $B^b_t$ of unit-face-value claims sold at price $Q^b_t$, raising
$Q^b_tB^b_t$ in real terms. In the disaster state $x_t=1$ a fraction $\Delta^b$ of the face value of maturing debt is repudiated; in the BAU state $x_t=0$ repayment is full. Using $R^b_t=(1-x_t\Delta^b)/Q^b_{t-1}$ from~\eqref{eq:A3_Rb}, real debt service on the maturing stock is
%
\begin{equation*}
  R^b_t\,Q^b_{t-1}B^b_{t-1}
  \;=\;\bigl(1-x_t\Delta^b\bigr)B^b_{t-1}
  \;=\;e^{x_t\ln(1-\Delta^b)}\,B^b_{t-1},
\end{equation*}
%
the last equality following from $x_t\in\{0,1\}$, which reconciles the two notations for the haircut used across this appendix. With no government purchases of goods, the flow budget balances every period:
%
\begin{equation}
  T_t = R^b_t Q^b_{t-1} B^b_{t-1} - Q^b_t B^b_t
  \tag{A.4.1}
\end{equation}
%
where $T_t$ denotes real lump-sum taxes. The outstanding quantity $B^b_t$ is
not set by a debt rule: it is determined residually by bank bond demand
$Q^b_tB^b_t=\tfrac{\phi}{1-\phi}Q_tS_t$ at the price
\eqref{eq:A3_bondprice}, with the government accommodating and $T_t$ adjusting
to satisfy \eqref{eq:A4_govBC}. Public saving is therefore not a state variable
and taxes are entirely endogenous.
 
The budget is balanced every period: tax revenues plus new bond issuance exactly cover the cost of outstanding debt service net of any disaster haircut. This eliminates public savings as a state variable and implies that taxes are entirely endogenous, adjusting to satisfy~\eqref{eq:A4_govBC} each period.
 
\textcite{Isore2017} write the government budget constraint without an explicit haircut term (their Appendix A.3 does not model partial default; the bond disaster term enters only through the household's bond Euler). In this paper, $e^{x_t \ln(1-\Delta^b)}$ appears implicitly in equation~\eqref{eq:A4_govBC} to record the fiscal cost of sovereign partial default to the government. With $\Delta^k > 0$, the capital stock is affected by disaster through the Gourio factor $(1-\Delta^k)^{x_t}$ in the accumulation equation and the government budget constraint records the loss on debt service through $e^{x_t\ln(1-\Delta^b)}$.

The nominal rate $r_t$ that appears in the constraint above is pinned down by the no-arbitrage condition~\eqref{eq:A4_fisher}, derived in paragraph~\ref{par:A17}; only the policy rule that sets it is stated here.
 

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Taylor Rule}
\label{par:A413}

Following \textcite{Isore2017}'s public-authority, it sets the nominal
interest rate $r_t$ according to a Taylor-type rule that targets deviations of inflation from its steady-state value $\bar{\pi}$ and of detrended output from its steady-state level $\bar{Y}$:

\begin{equation}
  r_t = \rho_r\, r_{t-1}
        + (1 - \rho_r)\!\left[
            \varphi_\pi\bigl(\pi_t - \bar{\pi}\bigr)
            + \varphi_Y\!\left(\frac{Y_t}{z_t} - \bar{Y}\right) + \bar{r}
          \right]
\tag{A.4.2}
\end{equation}

where $\rho_r\in(0,1)$ is the smoothing parameter, $\varphi_\pi>1$ the inflation response (Taylor principle), $\varphi_Y>0$ the output response, and $\bar r$ the steady-state nominal rate — renamed from $\phi_\pi,\phi_Y$ to avoid collision with the home-bias parameter $\phi$, and from the main text's $\lambda_\pi,\lambda_Y$ to avoid a second, worse collision with the bank leverage multiplier $\lambda_t$  of \ref{sec:banksov}. The output term is a level, not a log, deviation of
detrended output from its steady state, exactly as in \textcite{Isore2017}'s own specification; $\varphi_Y$ is calibrated against that same object, so the two are directly comparable without
rescaling. $r_t$'s relationship to the real risk-free rate $R^f_t$ is established in Appendix~\ref{par:A17}, following IS2017's own placement of the analogous condition in the household's problem.

The model's only live stochastic driver is the disaster-risk innovation $\varepsilon_{\theta,t}$: a monetary-policy shock $\varepsilon_{r,t}$ is declared in the exogenous block but held at zero standard deviation in the baseline calibration, and no productivity innovation is included at
all. The disaster probability $\theta_t$ does not enter the Taylor rule directly; it affects $r_t$ only through its general-equilibrium effects on $\pi_t$ and $Y_t/z_t$.


% ============================================================
\subsection{Model Closing Conditions}
\label{sec:A5}

% they close the model. Four markets require explicit clearing conditions: the labour market, the goods market, the deposit (bank liability) market and the public authority.
 
Market clearing conditions impose the requirement that all markets clear simultaneously in equilibrium. Together with the optimality conditions from A.1--A.4, that is the case if the goods and labour market is cleared. Subsequently, we will derive the good market and reinstate the labour, capital services, sovereign bond and loan market clearing conditions.  

\subsubsection{Good Market Clearing}
% 
Every financial claim in this model (loans, deposits, sovereign bonds, entrepreneur and bank net worth) is held by one agent and owed by another. This means that trading or accumulating these claims reallocates resources already counted once and neither creates nor destroys them. The aggregate resource constraint follows from going back only the flows that connect the financial layer to real production and consumption.
%
Firms pay out their revenue as wages, capital rental, and profit:
\begin{equation*}
  Y_t = w_t L_t + r_t\tilde{K}_t + \Pi_t^f.
\end{equation*}
Capital rental income accrues to entrepreneurs, who use it, together with new bank borrowing, to purchase capital from the capital-goods producer and repay existing loans (equations~\eqref{eq:A3_NE}--\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS}); every entrepreneur-side flow beyond the rental income itself is a financial claim matched by a counterparty and cancels in aggregate, once startup and exit transfers between households and entrepreneurs are treated as negligible following \textcite{Gertler2011}'s own convention. Banks are funded by deposits and net worth and hold loans and bonds as assets (equations~\eqref{eq:A3_bankBS}, \eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth}); the sovereign disaster haircut $\Delta^b$ redistributes resources from bondholders to the government rather than destroying them, and cancels against the identical term in the government budget constraint (A.4.1). The capital-goods producer's revenue from entrepreneurs' purchases nets against entrepreneurs' cost of that same purchase, leaving only its real resource use $I_t$, rebated to households as $\Pi_t^{cp}$ (equation~\eqref{eq:CP_profit}).

Eliminating $T_t$ as in \textcite{Isore2017}, we substitute the government budget constraint~\eqref{eq:A4_govBC} in real terms, dividing by $p_t$ yields
%
\begin{align*}
    T_t = \frac{B_t(1+r_{t-1})e^{x_t\ln(1-\Delta^b)}-B_{t+1}}{p_t}
\end{align*}
%
Using the bank's own bond pricing from \eqref{eq:A3_bondprice} and \eqref{eq:A3_Rb} to express it in the same unit as the bank already uses with the government's real financing identity is $T_t = R_t^bQ_{t-1}^bB_{t-1}^b - Q_t^bB_t^b$
%
Substituting these statements into the household budget ~\eqref{eq:budget} and substituting the bank balance sheet \eqref{eq:A3_bankBS} for $D_t = Q_tS_t + Q_t^bB_t^b - N_t^b$, the bond value term $Q_t^bB_t^b$ cancels completely so 
%
\begin{align*}
w_tL_t+R_{t-1}^dD_{t-1}+\Pi^f+\Pi_t^{cp} &= C_t +R_t^bQ_{t-1}^bB_{t-1}^b−Q_t^bB_t^b+D_t \\[4mm]
&= C_t +R_t^bQ_{t-1}^b B_{t-1}^b + Q_tS_t - N_t^b
\end{align*}
%
Further using the entrepreneur's balance sheet identity and net worth accumulation functions, we obtain a flow identity 

\begin{equation*}
\underbrace{R_t^K Q_{t-1}K_t^n}_{\text{return}} + \underbrace{Q_tS_t}_{\text{new loan}}
+ \iota_t^e
=
\underbrace{R_{t-1}^S Q_{t-1}S_{t-1}}_{\text{repay old loan}} + \underbrace{Q_tK_{t+1}}_{\text{buy new capital}}
+ \underbrace{(1-\sigma^e) \left[ R_t^KQ_{t-1}K_t^n - R_{t-1}^SQ_{t-1}S_{t-1} \right]}_{\text{exit transfer to households}}.
\end{equation*}

By the definition of the return on capital~\eqref{eq:A3_RK}, $R_t^KQ_{t-1}K_t^n = P_t^{k,\mathrm{real}}\tilde K_t
+ Q_t\left(1-\delta_0u_t^\eta\right)K_t$, which is the return on capital equal the rental income plus resale value and moreover by the capital-goods producer's profit identity from \ref{par:CP}, the net payment entrepreneurs make to the producer for trading old capital in for new equals investment expenditure plus capital-producer profits $I_t + \Pi_t^{cp}$. Hence the resale-and-repurchase pair collapses to a single real cost:
\begin{equation*}
P_t^{k,\mathrm{real}}\tilde K_t - I_t - \Pi_t^{cp} + Q_tS_t + \iota_t^e = R_{t-1}^SQ_{t-1}S_{t-1} + (1-\sigma^e) \left[ R_t^KQ_{t-1}K_t^n - R_{t-1}^SQ_{t-1}S_{t-1} \right].
\end{equation*}
%
Substituting it into the government and bank merging result from above, using the $R_{t-1}^KQ_{t-1}S_{t-1}$-type loan-income terms, which cancel against the bank's net-worth accumulation equation \eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth} in the same way the bond terms do. This yields
\begin{equation*}
C_t+I_t =
W_t^{\mathrm{real}}L_t + P_t^{k,\mathrm{real}}\tilde K_t + \Pi_t^f.
\end{equation*}


The transfer block $\Xi_t$ carried in the household budget cancels term by term against the exit and entry flows in \eqref{eq:A3_NE} and \eqref{eq:Bank_Net_worth}, so that
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A5_goods}
  Y_t=C_t+I_t \tag{A.5.1}
\end{equation}
%
holds exactly rather than as an approximation. The monitoring resources absorbed by the CSV contract do not appear separately, having been subsumed in the reduced-form premium \eqref{eq:A3_EFP} (paragraph~\ref{par:A313}).

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Divide through by $z_t$: $y_t = c_t + i_t$. \parencite[Eq.~A.38]{Isore2017} $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Labour and Capital Services Market}

 
Aggregate demand for labour and capital services from intermediate goods firms equals aggregate labour supply from households. Integrating the cost-minimisation condition across intermediate firms $j \in [0,1]$:
 
\begin{align}
  \int_0^1 L_{j,t}\,dj = L_t 
  \tag{A.5.2} \\[4mm]
  \int_0^1 \tilde{K}_{j,t}\,dj = \tilde{K}_t 
  \tag{A.5.3}
\end{align}
%
These conditions are already used in the cost minimisation process during the derivation of the aggregate production function \eqref{eq:cost_minimisation_int} where it eliminates the firm-level labour demand from the aggregate resource constraint. At the equilibrium level, they ensure that the real wage $W^{\text{real}}_t$ and the real rental rate $P^{k,\text{real}}_t$ adjust to clear the labour and capital services market. 
 
% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Hours $L_{j,t}$ are stationary (no trend); condition holds unchanged in the detrended system. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} $\tilde{K}_{j,t}$ inherits the trend of $K_t$; divide through by $Z_t$. $\to$ Appendix~B.
 
% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Sovereign Bond Market}

Sovereign bond market clearing follows directly from the home-bias identity~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias}, combined with the institutional fact that banks are the sole domestic holders of government debt, $B_t^b \equiv B_t$. 

% \noindent\textit{Stationarization flag:} Divide through by $z_t$. Both $Q^b_t B^b_t$ and $Q_tS_t$ grow at trend $z_t$; the ratio $\phi/(1-\phi)$ is dimensionless. Detrended form is identical. $\to$ Appendix~B.

% ------------------------------------------------------------
\paragraph{Loan Market}

The quantity $Q_tS_t$ appears on both sides of the credit market. From the entrepreneur's balance sheet~\eqref{eq:A3_entrep_BS}, $Q_tS_t = Q_tK_{t+1}-N^e_t$ is loan \emph{demand}, determined by the capital entrepreneurs wish to finance net of their own net worth. From the aggregated binding leverage constraint~\eqref{eq:A323} together with the home-bias identity~\eqref{eq:A3_homebias}, $Q_tS_t = (1-\phi)\lambda_t N^b_t$ is loan \emph{supply}, determined by bank net worth and the leverage limit. Their equality,
%
\begin{equation*}
  Q_tK_{t+1}-N^e_t \;=\; (1-\phi)\,\lambda_t\,N^b_t, \tag{A.5.4}
\end{equation*}
%
